# How protected is your medical information?

*Independent research compiled from public records for informational purposes only. Not legal, medical, or investment advice. Every concept states its BASIS, its ORIGIN, its THEME and the readers it was written for. The basis tiers are ranked, and never blended inside a single concept: a reader who accepts only `documented` entries can still rely on every one of those and discard the rest without unpicking anything. `testimony` is a dated first-person report, verified by nobody, and says so on its own face wherever it appears. Causes are reported as attributed, never asserted. See `meta/IS_META_terms.md`.*

**Basis: documented.** A source, ruling or official record supports this directly.  
**Origin: ai.** Derived by AI analysis of the dataset.  
**Theme: Surveillance and the person.**  
**Written for: Households and individuals · Legislators and regulators · Clinicians.**

**Not independently verified.**

**Series: Surveillance in everyday life, part 4 of 7.** 1. [Why does the camera on your door not answer to you?](IS_CON_whose-eyesight-is-it.md) · 2. [Who could refuse a system that saved their child?](IS_CON_prevention-as-the-product.md) · 3. [Does being watched change what people let themselves think?](IS_CON_no-private-thinking-space.md) · 4. How protected is your medical information? (this concept) · 5. [What are children subject to?](IS_CON_what-children-are-subject-to.md) · 6. [What happens to everyone around a target?](IS_CON_everyone-around-a-target.md) · 7. [Are people made into intelligence assets without knowing it?](IS_CON_made-into-assets-unknowing.md)

Less than most people assume, and the gap is structural rather than criminal. HIPAA protects a setting, not a category of information. It binds health plans, clearinghouses and providers who bill electronically. It does not bind most of the places health information is now generated. That distinction is not academic. When the Federal Trade Commission acted against GoodRx in February 2023, it could not use HIPAA at all — it used the Health Breach Notification Rule, and part of its complaint was that GoodRx had falsely suggested to consumers that it complied with HIPAA. What GoodRx had actually done was compile lists of users who bought particular medications and upload their email addresses, phone numbers and mobile advertising identifiers to Facebook, Google, Criteo, Branch and Twilio, so those users could be advertised to on the basis of their prescriptions and health conditions. The penalty was $1.5 million. The FTC brought a comparable action against BetterHelp over mental-health questionnaire data shared with advertisers. Scale is the other half. In 2024 a ransomware attack on Change Healthcare, a UnitedHealth subsidiary that processes a large share of American medical claims, exposed the data of roughly 190 million people — the largest health-data breach in United States history, and not a break-in at a doctor's office but a failure at a clearing house most patients had never heard of and none had chosen. So the honest answer is that medical privacy in America is strong where a clinician is involved and weak nearly everywhere else, and the largest single loss of it was not a violation by anyone treating a patient.

## Evidence

- HIPAA binds covered entities — plans, clearinghouses, and providers billing electronically — not most apps, wearables or websites that collect health information
- FTC v. GoodRx, February 2023: $1.5m civil penalty under the Health Breach Notification Rule, not HIPAA
- GoodRx uploaded email addresses, phone numbers and mobile advertising IDs of users of specific medications to Facebook, Google, Criteo, Branch and Twilio
- The FTC complaint stated GoodRx had falsely suggested to consumers that it complied with HIPAA
- The FTC brought a comparable action against BetterHelp over mental-health questionnaire data shared with advertisers
- 2024: a ransomware attack on Change Healthcare, a UnitedHealth subsidiary, exposed the records of roughly 190 million people — the largest US health-data breach on record

## What this does not answer

- These are enforcement actions and a breach. None of them establishes that any individual's records were accessed by any particular party for any particular purpose.
- A breach exposes data. It does not establish what was subsequently done with it, and in most cases that cannot be traced.
- This concept does not address law-enforcement access to medical records, which is governed by different rules and is a separate question.

## References

- [FTC enforcement action against GoodRx (2023)](https://www.ftc.gov/news-events/news/press-releases/2023/02/ftc-enforcement-action-bar-goodrx-sharing-consumers-sensitive-health-info-advertising)
- [What happens to everyone around a target?](/concepts#everyone-around-a-target)
- [Are people made into intelligence assets without knowing it?](/concepts#made-into-assets-unknowing)

*The FTC is cited for its own enforcement action. The linked concepts describe adjacent collection markets and neither corroborates this one.*

## Scope

This concept reports enforcement actions and a publicly disclosed breach. It establishes no unlawful access to any individual's records, and no conduct by any clinician or care provider.

# How small is nanotechnology, and what can it do today?

*Independent research compiled from public records for informational purposes only. Not legal, medical, or investment advice. Every concept states its BASIS, its ORIGIN, its THEME and the readers it was written for. The basis tiers are ranked, and never blended inside a single concept: a reader who accepts only `documented` entries can still rely on every one of those and discard the rest without unpicking anything. `testimony` is a dated first-person report, verified by nobody, and says so on its own face wherever it appears. Causes are reported as attributed, never asserted. See `meta/IS_META_terms.md`.*

**Basis: documented.** A source, ruling or official record supports this directly.  
**Origin: ai.** Derived by AI analysis of the dataset.  
**Theme: Neurotechnology.**  
**Written for: Households and individuals · Press and researchers.**

**Not independently verified.**

[Nanotechnology](/glossary/nanotechnology) works at roughly 1 to 100 nanometres. A nanometre is one billionth of a metre. A sheet of paper is about 100,000 nanometres thick.

A ladder of sizes helps:

- **A sheet of [graphene](/glossary/graphene):** one atom thick, a fraction of a nanometre.
- **A [lipid nanoparticle](/glossary/lipid-nanoparticle) in an mRNA vaccine:** roughly 100 nanometres, about the size of a virus.
- **A neuron's cell body:** thousands to tens of thousands of nanometres across.
- **A sheet of paper:** about 100,000 nanometres.
- **A 2016 [neural dust](/glossary/neural-dust) sensor:** about 3 millimetres long, or 3,000,000 nanometres.

The last line is the point most often missed. Most of what is called nanotechnology in brain research is a device of ordinary, visible size built from nanomaterials: graphene electrodes, nanowire mesh, a millimetre-scale sensor. The parts that are truly nanoscale are particles, and a particle on its own cannot compute, store or transmit anything.

What nanoscale technology does today, on the record:

- **Carries medicines and genetic instructions into cells,** as in mRNA vaccines and other approved nanomedicines.
- **Makes electrodes thinner, softer or transparent,** as in [transparent brain-computer interfaces](/glossary/transparent-brain-computer-interfaces-bcis) and graphene arrays now being tested in people during surgery.
- **Converts energy sent from outside the body into a local effect,** in laboratories and animals: [magnetoelectric nanoparticles](/glossary/magnetoelectric-nanoparticles) respond to a magnetic field, and in [optogenetics](/glossary/optogenetics) upconversion nanoparticles turn near-infrared light into visible light. In both cases the power and the instruction come from equipment outside the body.

What is only proposed: nanoscale robots that travel through the bloodstream, position themselves among brain cells and link the brain to a network. That idea has been published as a proposal (see [What is a brain/cloud interface?](/concepts/brain-cloud-interface)). Nothing of the kind has been built.

## Evidence

- Nanoscale is roughly 1 to 100 nanometres; a sheet of paper is about 100,000 nanometres thick (U.S. National Nanotechnology Initiative)
- Neural dust motes demonstrated in 2016 were about 3 millimetres long (UC Berkeley)
- Magnetoelectric nanoparticles and upconversion nanoparticles act only when driven by an external magnetic field or light source
- Brain-linked nanorobots exist only as a published proposal (Frontiers in Neuroscience, 2019)

## What this does not answer

- Size alone does not set a limit on what future devices could do. This concept describes what is on the record now.

## References

- [Just How Small Is Nano? — U.S. National Nanotechnology Initiative](https://www.nano.gov/about-nanotechnology/just-how-small-is-nano)
- [Sprinkling of neural dust opens door to electroceuticals — Berkeley News (2016)](https://news.berkeley.edu/2016/08/03/sprinkling-of-neural-dust-opens-door-to-electroceuticals/)
- [Near-infrared deep brain stimulation via upconversion nanoparticles — RIKEN (2018)](https://www.riken.jp/en/news_pubs/research_news/pr/2018/20180209_1/)
- [Did anyone try to build a way in without surgery?](/concepts/nonsurgical-by-design)

*Each source is cited for the measurement or result it reports.*

## Scope

This concept summarises published sizes and demonstrated capabilities. It does not predict what future devices will or will not do.

id: IS-CON-LAW-FOR-NEURAL-DATA
title: Concept — Why did legislatures write laws for neural data?
collection: concepts

# Why did legislatures write laws for neural data?

*Independent research compiled from public records for informational purposes only. Not legal, medical, or investment advice. Every concept states its BASIS, its ORIGIN, its THEME and the readers it was written for. The basis tiers are ranked, and never blended inside a single concept: a reader who accepts only `documented` entries can still rely on every one of those and discard the rest without unpicking anything. `testimony` is a dated first-person report, verified by nobody, and says so on its own face wherever it appears. Causes are reported as attributed, never asserted. See `meta/IS_META_terms.md`.*

**Basis: documented.** A source, ruling or official record supports this directly.  
**Origin: ai.** Derived by AI analysis of the dataset.  
**Theme: Neurotechnology.**  
**Written for: Legislators and regulators.**

**Not independently verified.**

**Series: Neurotechnology and neural data, part 5 of 6.** 1. [Who owns what your brain emits?](IS_CON_who-owns-neural-data.md) · 2. [Can a machine read what you are thinking?](IS_CON_can-a-machine-read-thought.md) · 3. [Did anyone try to build a way in without surgery?](IS_CON_nonsurgical-by-design.md) · 4. [What would it actually take to do this without consent?](IS_CON_what-it-would-take.md) · 5. Why did legislatures write laws for neural data? (this concept) · 6. [Are children harmed by wireless exposure?](IS_CON_children-wearables-and-rf.md)

Legislatures rarely move early. On neural data, three of them did. Colorado passed HB24-1058 in 2024, amending its consumer privacy act to require express consent before neural data is collected or used, separate consent or an opt-out before it goes to a third party, and a route for a person to have it deleted. California did the same through SB 1223, folding neural data into the categories its privacy act treats as sensitive. Montana went further from a different direction, adding neural data to its genetic information privacy act, effective October 2025. What is notable is not the content but the margins: these passed unanimously or nearly so, in a period when almost nothing does. A category of information most people have never heard of was given statutory protection by bipartisan votes in three states. Either those legislatures were persuaded that a capability exists worth regulating, or they were persuaded one is close enough that waiting was the greater risk. The record shows the votes. It does not show which of those two it was.

## Evidence

- Colorado HB24-1058 (2024): express consent to collect or use neural data; separate consent or opt-out for third-party disclosure; deletion route
- California SB 1223: neural data added to the sensitive categories of the state consumer privacy act
- Montana LC0005, effective October 2025: neural data added to the state genetic information privacy act
- All three passed unanimously or near-unanimously

## What this does not answer

- A law existing does not establish that the harm it anticipates has occurred. Legislatures also regulate in advance.
- None of the three statutes names a specific incident as its cause, so the record cannot say what persuaded the votes.
- This concept does not address whether the definitions these laws use are technically adequate, which is itself contested.

## References

- [Colorado HB24-1058 — bill text](https://content.leg.colorado.gov/sites/default/files/documents/2024A/bills/2024a_1058_01.pdf)
- [States pass privacy laws to protect brain data — KFF Health News](https://kffhealthnews.org/mental-health/colorado-california-montana-states-neural-data-privacy-laws-neurorights/)
- [Who owns what your brain emits?](/concepts#who-owns-neural-data)
- [We cannot prove which came first, the law or the system](/concepts#sequence-cannot-be-proven)

*The last link is method, and it applies directly here: this record cannot establish whether law followed capability or anticipated it.*

## Scope

This concept reports enacted legislation. It does not establish that any neural data harm has occurred in any of the three states, nor what motivated any legislator's vote.

# A law saying “keep it local” doesn’t keep it local

*Independent research compiled from public records for informational purposes only. Not legal, medical, or investment advice. Every concept states its BASIS, its ORIGIN, its THEME and the readers it was written for. The basis tiers are ranked, and never blended inside a single concept: a reader who accepts only `documented` entries can still rely on every one of those and discard the rest without unpicking anything. `testimony` is a dated first-person report, verified by nobody, and says so on its own face wherever it appears. Causes are reported as attributed, never asserted. See `meta/IS_META_terms.md`.*

**Basis: structural.** This follows from what the dataset does or does not contain.  
**Origin: ai.** Derived by AI analysis of the dataset.  
**Theme: Procurement and accountability.**  
**Written for: Legislators and regulators.**

**Series: Government cloud: what the contracts show, part 2 of 7.** 1. [The headline spending figure is not what governments spent](IS_CON_headline-spending-is-not-spending.md) · 2. A law saying “keep it local” doesn’t keep it local (this concept) · 3. [Systems built for an emergency get switched off after it](IS_CON_emergency-systems-withdrawn.md) · 4. [A regulator finding does not stop a deployment](IS_CON_findings-dont-stop-deployment.md) · 5. [Fined in Europe, hired in America](IS_CON_fined-in-europe-hired-in-america.md) · 6. [Accountability isn't wired to deployment, even in the schema](IS_CON_accountability-not-wired.md) · 7. [There is no column for you](IS_CON_no-column-for-you.md)

Twenty countries in this record have rules requiring government data to stay within their borders. In ten of the countries where we can see actual deployments, most government workloads still run on American companies anyway. The only places where that genuinely changes are the ones that shut those companies out altogether — and even there, the few remaining records are exits rather than operations.

## Evidence

- 44 of 99 regulations carry a localisation requirement, across 20 geographies
- US-headquartered vendors still hold the majority in 10 of them — Australia 25 of 26, Israel 15 of 15, Netherlands 7 of 7, Denmark 4 of 4
- Displacement only under explicit exclusion: China 3 of 28, Russia 3 of 23 — and all three Russian records are decommissioned
- 34 of 107 vendors are US-based, but they hold 273 of 399 deployments

[The fentanyl reversal](IS_CON_the-fentanyl-reversal.md) · 3. The numbers under the numbers (this concept) · 4. [Prescribing is not a measure of illness](IS_CON_prescribing-is-not-prevalence.md) · 5. [Are children harmed by wireless exposure?](IS_CON_children-wearables-and-rf.md)

**Series: How to read this archive, part 5 of 8.** 1. [Next to each other is not because of each other](IS_CON_co-occurrence-is-not-cause.md) · 2. [We cannot prove which came first, the law or the system](IS_CON_sequence-cannot-be-proven.md) · 3. [“Official” is not the same as “independent”](IS_CON_official-is-not-independent.md) · 4. [The headline spending figure is not what governments spent](IS_CON_headline-spending-is-not-spending.md) · 5. The numbers under the numbers (this concept) · 6. [Prescribing is not a measure of illness](IS_CON_prescribing-is-not-prevalence.md) · 7. [Why isn't any of this in the news?](IS_CON_why-isnt-this-in-the-news.md) · 8. [There is no column for you](IS_CON_no-column-for-you.md)

A country reporting few suicides may have few suicides, or may not be counting them. The West Bank & Gaza record 0.65 deaths per 100,000 — which would be the lowest rate on earth by a wide margin, and much more plausibly measures a fragmented registration system in a region where the death is heavily stigmatised. Russia's falling rate runs alongside a rising share of deaths filed as “undetermined intent”. India's official figures are police reports; verbal-autopsy studies find substantially more. In at least 24 countries suicide or its attempt is a criminal matter, which suppresses both help-seeking and recording. WHO's own position is that most member states lack vital registration good enough for this purpose, and that roughly one suicide in six goes missing worldwide — one in three in lower-income countries. The register that documents this is not a footnote to the chart. It is the finding: a low number is sometimes a fact about a country, and sometimes a fact about its filing.

## Evidence

- 15 rows in the data-quality register, each naming a mechanism and a source
- Russia: rising 'undetermined intent' share masks suicides
- India: police-reported NCRB figures against verbal-autopsy and GBD estimates
- UK deliberately INCLUDES undetermined-intent deaths — the opposite convention
- ≥24 countries criminalise suicide or attempts; ~1 in 6 missing globally

## What this does not answer

- The register cannot say how much of any single country's trend is real and how much is recording. It documents that both are present.

## References

- [How much the numbers can be trusted](/data)

*The data-quality register lists every mechanism with its source.*

# Are people made into intelligence assets without knowing it?

*Independent research compiled from public records for informational purposes only. Not legal, medical, or investment advice. Every concept states its BASIS, its ORIGIN, its THEME and the readers it was written for. The basis tiers are ranked, and never blended inside a single concept: a reader who accepts only `documented` entries can still rely on every one of those and discard the rest without unpicking anything. `testimony` is a dated first-person report, verified by nobody, and says so on its own face wherever it appears. Causes are reported as attributed, never asserted. See `meta/IS_META_terms.md`.*

**Basis: documented.** A source, ruling or official record supports this directly.  
**Origin: author.** The author's own observation, from experience.  
**Theme: Surveillance and the person.**  
**Written for: Households and individuals · Law enforcement and investigators.**

**Not independently verified.**

**Series: Harassment and coercion, part 6 of 8.** 1. [Organised covert harassment of individuals is established fact](IS_CON_organised-harassment-is-fact.md) · 2. [Zersetzung's methods are crimes](IS_CON_zersetzung-methods-are-crimes.md) · 3. [We're keeping you to ourselves](IS_CON_ruin-first-then-rescue.md) · 4. [An attack to force acknowledgment](IS_CON_attack-to-force-acknowledgment.md) · 5. [What happens to everyone around a target?](IS_CON_everyone-around-a-target.md) · 6. Are people made into intelligence assets without knowing it? (this concept) · 7. [Does the explanation itself do harm?](IS_CON_explanation-is-part-of-the-harm.md) · 8. [Are Denver citizens subject to acoustic weapons?](IS_CON_denver-acoustic-weapons.md)

**Series: Surveillance in everyday life, part 7 of 7.** 1. [Why does the camera on your door not answer to you?](IS_CON_whose-eyesight-is-it.md) · 2. [Who could refuse a system that saved their child?](IS_CON_prevention-as-the-product.md) · 3. [Does being watched change what people let themselves think?](IS_CON_no-private-thinking-space.md) · 4. [How protected is your medical information?](IS_CON_how-protected-is-your-medical-record.md) · 5. [What are children subject to?](IS_CON_what-children-are-subject-to.md) · 6. [What happens to everyone around a target?](IS_CON_everyone-around-a-target.md) · 7. Are people made into intelligence assets without knowing it? (this concept)

Intelligence tradecraft has always separated a witting source from an unwitting one. A person can supply information without knowing who receives it, or that anyone does. What changed is scale, and it required nobody's cooperation. American law enforcement agencies buy location data that phones emit continuously. The Electronic Frontier Foundation's investigation into Fog Data Science documented a company selling local police searchable access to billions of location signals harvested from ordinary apps, at prices small departments could afford. Babel Street's Locate X offered comparable capability, and EPIC obtained records of Customs and Border Protection's use of it. The mechanism is commercial: brokers buy from the advertising ecosystem and agencies buy from brokers. No warrant is involved because no compulsion is involved. The result is a population of unwitting sources. A person carrying a phone generates a record of where they went, who they were near and for how long, and that record is purchasable. They were never approached, never recruited, and are never harassed — because harassment would defeat the purpose. The value of an unwitting asset lies precisely in their not knowing. What this does not describe is access to perception. No documented capability reads a person's eyesight, and the mechanism above does not require one. What people already emit is sufficient.

## Evidence

- EFF investigation into Fog Data Science (2022): searchable location data sold to local police, drawn from billions of signals emitted by ordinary apps
- EPIC obtained FOIA records covering Customs and Border Protection's use of Babel Street's Locate X
- The purchase route avoids the warrant requirement because it involves no compulsion — the data is bought, not seized
- Witting versus unwitting source is a standing distinction in intelligence tradecraft, not a novel category

## What this does not answer

- The record establishes commercial purchase of bulk location data. It does not establish any programme of deliberate individual targeting.
- No documented capability accesses a person's visual perception, and none is needed for the collection described here.
- Whether any particular person's data has been purchased by any particular agency is not answerable from public records.

## References

- [Inside Fog Data Science — EFF](https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2022/08/inside-fog-data-science-secretive-company-selling-mass-surveillance-local-police)
- [CBP and Babel Street Locate X — EPIC FOIA](https://epic.org/documents/epic-foia-cbp-babel-street-location-tracking-service/)
- [There is no column for you](/concepts#no-column-for-you)

*EFF and EPIC are cited for their own documented investigations. The linked concept is method — it records that the person a system is used on appears almost nowhere in the procurement record.*

## Scope

This concept reports documented commercial data sales to law enforcement. It establishes no programme of individual targeting, no access to perception, and no conduct by any named agency beyond what the cited investigations found.

# Does being watched change what people let themselves think?

*Independent research compiled from public records for informational purposes only. Not legal, medical, or investment advice. Every concept states its BASIS, its ORIGIN, its THEME and the readers it was written for. The basis tiers are ranked, and never blended inside a single concept: a reader who accepts only `documented` entries can still rely on every one of those and discard the rest without unpicking anything. `testimony` is a dated first-person report, verified by nobody, and says so on its own face wherever it appears. Causes are reported as attributed, never asserted. See `meta/IS_META_terms.md`.*

**Basis: documented.** A source, ruling or official record supports this directly.  
**Origin: author.** The author's own observation, from experience.  
**Theme: Surveillance and the person.**  
**Written for: Press and researchers.**

**Not independently verified.**

**Series: Surveillance in everyday life, part 3 of 7.** 1. [Why does the camera on your door not answer to you?](IS_CON_whose-eyesight-is-it.md) · 2. [Who could refuse a system that saved their child?](IS_CON_prevention-as-the-product.md) · 3. Does being watched change what people let themselves think? (this concept) · 4. [How protected is your medical information?](IS_CON_how-protected-is-your-medical-record.md) · 5. [What are children subject to?](IS_CON_what-children-are-subject-to.md) · 6. [What happens to everyone around a target?](IS_CON_everyone-around-a-target.md) · 7. [Are people made into intelligence assets without knowing it?](IS_CON_made-into-assets-unknowing.md)

Amnesty International's 2023 report Automated Apartheid documented facial-recognition systems, Red Wolf and Blue Wolf among them, used to control Palestinian movement in the occupied territories, with residents describing repeated identification at checkpoints as a condition of ordinary life. Palestinians interviewed described the effect in consistent and non-technical terms: there was no space left in which to think privately. That effect is measurable, and it has been measured in the United States. Jonathon Penney, writing in the Berkeley Technology Law Journal in 2016, examined Wikipedia traffic to privacy-sensitive articles before and after June 2013, when the NSA and PRISM disclosures became public. He found a statistically significant immediate decline, with evidence that it persisted. People stopped looking things up. Nobody instructed them to. The migration of such tools is documented too. Julian Go, in the American Journal of Sociology, traces how instruments and doctrines developed for imperial control returned to domestic American policing; cell-site simulators reached local departments from military origins by the same route. The pattern is old enough to carry a name in the literature. So the question worth asking is not whether America has some particular system. It is narrower and answerable: given that capabilities move from conflict territory to domestic policing, and that surveillance measurably changes what people do, what has already arrived here, and what has it already changed?

## Evidence

- Amnesty International, Automated Apartheid (2023): Red Wolf and Blue Wolf facial recognition used to control Palestinian movement in the OPT
- Penney, 'Chilling Effects: Online Surveillance and Wikipedia Use', Berkeley Technology Law Journal 31:1 (2016) — statistically significant immediate decline in privacy-sensitive article traffic after June 2013, with persistence
- Go, 'The Imperial Origins of American Policing', American Journal of Sociology 125:5 — instruments of imperial control returning to domestic policing
- Cell-site simulators reached local US police departments from military origins

## What this does not answer

- Naming a documented migration does not establish that any specific system has migrated. It establishes that the route exists and has been used before.
- Penney measured behaviour, not thought. What a person looks up is observable; what they think is not, and no study here claims otherwise.
- Nothing in this record establishes any capability to observe conversation directly, in any territory.

## References

- [Automated Apartheid — Amnesty International](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2023/05/israel-opt-israeli-authorities-are-using-facial-recognition-technology-to-entrench-apartheid/)
- [Chilling Effects: Online Surveillance and Wikipedia Use — Penney (2016)](https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=2769645)
- [The Imperial Origins of American Policing — American Journal of Sociology](https://www.journals.uchicago.edu/doi/10.1086/708464)

*Each source is cited for its own finding. Amnesty documents one territory, Penney measures one population's behaviour, and Go describes a historical route. None of the three corroborates either of the others, and together they do not establish a present-day American system.*

## Scope

This concept reports published research and human-rights documentation. It does not establish that any system documented in one territory operates in another, nor that any capability exists to observe thought or conversation directly.

# Did anyone try to build a way in without surgery?

*Independent research compiled from public records for informational purposes only. Not legal, medical, or investment advice. Every concept states its BASIS, its ORIGIN, its THEME and the readers it was written for. The basis tiers are ranked, and never blended inside a single concept: a reader who accepts only `documented` entries can still rely on every one of those and discard the rest without unpicking anything. `testimony` is a dated first-person report, verified by nobody, and says so on its own face wherever it appears. Causes are reported as attributed, never asserted. See `meta/IS_META_terms.md`.*

**Basis: documented.** A source, ruling or official record supports this directly.  
**Origin: ai.** Derived by AI analysis of the dataset.  
**Theme: Neurotechnology.**  
**Written for: Legislators and regulators.**

**Not independently verified.**

**Series: Neurotechnology and neural data, part 3 of 6.** 1. [Who owns what your brain emits?](IS_CON_who-owns-neural-data.md) · 2. [Can a machine read what you are thinking?](IS_CON_can-a-machine-read-thought.md) · 3. Did anyone try to build a way in without surgery? (this concept) · 4. [What would it actually take to do this without consent?](IS_CON_what-it-would-take.md) · 5. [Why did legislatures write laws for neural data?](IS_CON_law-for-neural-data.md) · 6. [Are children harmed by wireless exposure?](IS_CON_children-wearables-and-rf.md)

Yes, openly, and the programme documents say so. DARPA's Next-Generation Nonsurgical Neurotechnology programme — N3 — set out, in its own words, to develop high-performance bi-directional brain-machine interfaces for able-bodied service members. Bi-directional means read and write. Able-bodied means the purpose was not restoring lost function; the stated applications were controlling unmanned vehicles and cyber-defence systems. Six teams were funded in 2019. The published performance targets were specific: sixteen independent channels, within sixteen cubic millimetres of neural tissue, at fifty milliseconds of latency, using light, acoustic or electromagnetic energy rather than implanted electrodes. One funded team, led by Battelle, took a route between surgery and none. Its project, BrainSTORMS, proposed injecting [magnetoelectric nanoparticles](/glossary/magnetoelectric-nanoparticles), guiding them to a region of the brain, and reading from and writing to them through a helmet. That is the programme's clearest link to nanotechnology. It is still an injection, and it was a research proposal, not a system shown working in a person. The programme is now listed as complete and retained for reference. What this establishes is intent and investment, publicly recorded. It does not establish that the targets were met, and the targets themselves describe a person wearing equipment, not a person at a distance.

## Evidence

- DARPA N3 stated aim: 'high-performance, bi-directional brain-machine interfaces for able-bodied service members'
- Named applications: unmanned vehicle control and cyber defence — not clinical restoration
- Published targets: 16 independent channels, 16mm³ of tissue, 50ms latency
- Modalities pursued: light, acoustic and electromagnetic energy, rather than implanted electrodes
- Six teams funded from 2019; programme now listed as complete
- One team (Battelle, BrainSTORMS) proposed injected magnetoelectric nanotransducers read and written through a helmet

## What this does not answer

- A funded programme with published targets is evidence of intent, not of achievement. DARPA funds many things that do not work.
- The targets describe a wearable interface on a consenting operator. Nothing in the programme description concerns action at a distance or without consent.
- Whether any target was met is not established by the programme page, and the results are not reported there.

## References

- [N3: Next-Generation Nonsurgical Neurotechnology — DARPA](https://www.darpa.mil/research/programs/next-generation-nonsurgical-neurotechnology)
- [Battelle to develop injectable bi-directional brain-computer interface — Medical Design Briefs](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/34496-battelle-to-develop-injectable-bi-directional-brain-computer-interface)
- [What would it actually take to do this without consent?](/concepts#what-it-would-take)
- [Can a machine read what you are thinking?](/concepts#can-a-machine-read-thought)

*DARPA is cited for its own published programme description. That a goal was funded is not evidence the goal was reached.*

## Scope

This concept reports a publicly documented research programme and its stated objectives. It does not establish that any capability was achieved, deployed, or used on any person.

# “Only I can hear it” is not, by itself, unusual

*Independent research compiled from public records for informational purposes only. Not legal, medical, or investment advice. Every concept states its BASIS, its ORIGIN, its THEME and the readers it was written for. The basis tiers are ranked, and never blended inside a single concept: a reader who accepts only `documented` entries can still rely on every one of those and discard the rest without unpicking anything. `testimony` is a dated first-person report, verified by nobody, and says so on its own face wherever it appears. Causes are reported as attributed, never asserted. See `meta/IS_META_terms.md`.*

**Basis: documented.** A source, ruling or official record supports this directly.  
**Origin: ai.** Derived by AI analysis of the dataset.  
**Theme: Neurotechnology.**  
**Written for: Households and individuals · Clinicians · Press and researchers.**

**Series: Hearing something no one else hears, part 1 of 5.** 1. “Only I can hear it” is not, by itself, unusual (this concept) · 2. [Can you record it?](IS_CON_can-you-record-it.md) · 3. [Your house is not haunted](IS_CON_what-produces-the-feeling.md) · 4. [Are Denver citizens subject to acoustic weapons?](IS_CON_denver-acoustic-weapons.md) · 5. [Does the explanation itself do harm?](IS_CON_explanation-is-part-of-the-harm.md)

A sound that one person hears and the person beside them does not is often treated — by the person experiencing it, and by whoever they tell — as the strangest part of the account, and therefore as the part that demands an exotic explanation. It is the opposite. High-frequency hearing declines with age, steadily and in everyone, and the highest frequencies go first. A tone can sit precisely where one person hears it easily and another, ten years older, hears nothing at all. This is ordinary biology with a name, and it has been built into a commercial product deployed on public streets for two decades. None of this establishes what any particular person has heard. It establishes something narrower and more useful: that this specific feature of an account — that others did not hear it — carries far less weight as evidence than it seems to, and should not be the thing that convinces anyone, in either direction.

## Evidence

- Presbycusis: in 162 adults aged 21 to 70, thresholds at 16 kHz averaged about 25 dB HL at ages 21–30 and 50–55 dB HL from 31 to 60. In the 61–70 group, fewer than one in five responded at 16 kHz and none at 18 kHz.
- The Mosquito, sold since 2005, emits roughly 17.4 kHz for exactly this reason — to be unbearable to younger people and unnoticed by older ones. Deployed on streets in the UK, Canada, Australia, Belgium, France and Ireland.
- The Council of Europe’s Parliamentary Assembly called on governments to ban it in 2010; the UN Committee on the Rights of the Child called on the UK to ban it in 2016. The Home Office declined. No national statutory ban has been enacted anywhere.
- The vendor’s own framing overstates the effect: hearing loss at these frequencies is a continuous slope, not a cutoff at 25.

## What this does not answer

- Is the sound at a frequency a younger person would hear and an older one would not — and were the people who heard nothing older?
- Has anyone tried a recorder capable of the frequency, rather than a second pair of ears?
- If the sound carries words rather than a tone, does presbycusis explain it at all?

## References

- [Presbycusis](/glossary/presbycusis)
- [The Mosquito](/glossary/mosquito-device)
- [Sampling limit](/glossary/sampling-limit)
- [Can you record it?](/concepts#can-you-record-it)

*The third question above is the important one, and this concept does not answer it. Age-related hearing loss explains a tone that some people miss. It does not explain speech.*

## Scope

This concept narrows what one feature of an account can be taken to show. It makes no finding about what anyone has experienced.

# Who protects you?

*Independent research compiled from public records for informational purposes only. Not legal, medical, or investment advice. Every concept states its BASIS, its ORIGIN, its THEME and the readers it was written for. The basis tiers are ranked, and never blended inside a single concept: a reader who accepts only `documented` entries can still rely on every one of those and discard the rest without unpicking anything. `testimony` is a dated first-person report, verified by nobody, and says so on its own face wherever it appears. Causes are reported as attributed, never asserted. See `meta/IS_META_terms.md`.*

**Basis: pattern.** An observation drawn from experience, offered as an observation.  
**Origin: author.** The author's own observation, from experience.  
**Theme: Reported experience.**  
**Written for: Households and individuals · Law enforcement and investigators · Legislators and regulators.**

**Not independently verified.**

*This concept is the author's research into his own Personal Protection Plan. It draws on that document, on his Asset Protection Comparison research, on his own experience and on the statements recorded in the journal. It is not medical, legal or psychological advice, and nobody here can tell you what is happening to you.*

**You protect you.** No plan, product or person can protect you the way your own choices can. The author wrote a [Personal Protection Plan](https://docs.google.com/document/d/1kY_452-jTwhXpABLDKvv8mc0-PE56-6iPPk5BpKtbF8/), a research document kept in the Documents section; this concept asks what it can actually do to help you protect yourself: what you can do for free, what costs money, and what no plan can do. If you live with others, start with [What can a household do to protect itself?](/concepts/what-can-a-household-do)

## The short answer

If this might be happening to you, you may not be able to stop it on your own. What you can do is stay safe, keep a record, and keep your options open, and most of what helps is free. The things that cost money can document what may be happening; none of them can make it stop.

Communication like this, speech, sounds or sensations that a person didn't ask for and can't trace, has not been publicly acknowledged or explained by any government, agency or health authority, as far as Invisible Ships knows. There are technological explanations for it, and [long-range acoustic devices (LRAD)](/glossary/long-range-acoustic-device) are one of them; [neurotechnology](/glossary/neurotechnology) is another, and the two may overlap. Whichever it is, months of harassment, threats, pressure to die and attempts to get into someone's home are criminal either way.

Health care has no recognised explanation for communication like this, so the explanation people are usually offered is a psychiatric one, such as [schizophrenia](/glossary/schizophrenia). No medical test confirms that diagnosis. That is why this site asks everyone to speak only for themselves, and never diagnoses anyone.

*On the record:* between 2000 and 2021 the suicide rate in the United States rose 39.9%, while the world's fell 27% (see [The world's suicide rate fell. The United States' rose.](/concepts/us-rose-against-the-trend)). Nothing establishes a link between that rise and the communication described here.

## What you can do for free

### Never let anyone in, and never reward it

These are the rules the author lives by. In his experience, and in the statements recorded in the journal, breaking any one of them could cost you your life.

- **Never open your door, or let anyone into your home, because a voice or a communication asks you to.** The journal records statements such as "Open your door for the spirit world," including in the middle of the night.
- **Never go outside to meet someone because you were asked to,** for example "walk three blocks west."
- **If you live with children or roommates,** tell them never to open the door or go outside because of something they hear.
- **Never reward the communication.** It doesn't reach you by telephone or email; it may be reaching you through long-range technology, without your consent, and if so, that is criminal. Don't do what it asks, don't bargain with it, and don't accept what it offers. It may tempt you: recorded statements describe a [military "ugly workflow"](/glossary/military-ugly-workflow), offers of money, intimacy, drugs, relief from suffering, a job or a family, made to get you to open the door or go outside.
- **Never break the law because someone suggests it or pressures you to,** and never harm yourself or anyone else, whatever a communication says.
- In an emergency, call 911. If you're struggling, call or text 988 in the US, or find a free helpline at findahelpline.com.

### If someone approaches you

Someone you don't know might walk up to you and say something like *"We know what you're going through. Will you sit down and talk to us?"* In the author's view, that may be a life-threatening situation, even in public.

- **Let them speak first.** Don't start the conversation. It gives a real police officer or crisis worker the chance to say who they are.
- **With a stranger, use the three-second rule:** don't engage for more than about three seconds. "No, thank you," without breaking stride, is safer than asking who they are. Walk towards other people. (From the author's Asset Protection Comparison research.)
- **Don't sit down, don't get into a vehicle, and don't go anywhere with them.**
- **With someone official,** such as a police officer or a crisis team, ask for identification, stay standing, and call 911 to confirm who they are if you're unsure. This is where a no-contact card helps.
- **Afterwards,** write down the date, the time, the place and what was said.
- **Before you share anything with anyone, or if someone asks to meet you,** see [What does safe sharing look like?](/concepts/safe-sharing)

### A no-contact card

A small card you carry and can hand to someone official, so you don't have to explain yourself under pressure. Print it at home:

> **I do not consent** to contact, to anyone entering my home, or to any forced or coerced intervention.
>
> I am keeping a dated record, and this conversation may be recorded.
>
> **Please contact:** [a lawyer or trusted person, and their phone number]
>
> I have a psychiatric advance directive: [where it is kept]
>
> In an emergency, call 911.

A card has no legal force on its own. In Colorado, police and clinicians can still act in an emergency if they believe someone is in danger. What it does is show, calmly and on the record, that you don't consent, and who should be called.

### A psychiatric advance directive

*On the record:* since 2019, Colorado law (HB19-1044) has let you write a psychiatric advance directive: a legal document, made in advance, saying what mental health care you do and don't want in a crisis, and who should speak for you if you can't. Mental Health Colorado offers it free. It is the lawful way to say what a no-contact card can only point to.

### Keep a dated record

A record made at the time is worth more than a memory, whatever the cause turns out to be. The plan's advice is to **note the exact time** each thing happens, so it can later be compared with anything a technical check finds.

- Say the date, the time and where you are at the start of each recording.
- Before repeating what you hear, say "From the distance". Before your own words, say "From me".
- Keep what you heard separate from what you think it means.
- Never edit the original, and keep your own copy.

### The competency baseline video

The plan's first step is a dated video of yourself, calm, reading a statement in your own words. If anyone ever tries to force or coerce a diagnosis on you, such as schizophrenia, you would have a recording of yourself speaking clearly and calmly at the time. That may be some of the strongest evidence you have. The author recorded his on 20 January 2026.

**Keep it to yourself.** There is value in not sharing it: store it somewhere safe that only you control, keep a copy, and share it only with a lawyer or someone you trust, if and when it's needed.

Here's what our research suggests. There are two versions of the script, and this concept doesn't recommend one over the other.

**The original script**

> "Today is [date]. My name is [your name]. I am recording this to establish my sound mind and legal intent. I am currently documenting unconsented neurological battery in [city]. I am not suicidal, I am not delusional, and I explicitly reject all 'wellness interventions' or forced medication. This video serves as evidence that I am a competent witness to a crime in progress."

**An alternative script**

> "Today is [date], and the time is [time]. My name is [your name], and I'm in [city]. I'm recording this to keep a dated record of my own words and state of mind. I'm documenting communication I didn't consent to, and harassment, which began around [month and year]. I'm calm, and I'm describing what I've experienced as accurately as I can. If I'm ever unable to speak for myself, I'd like [a trusted person or lawyer] to be contacted, and my psychiatric advance directive to be followed. I'm making this recording freely."

*Both scripts were developed with AI, and neither is advice. You know your situation best. Take your time, change the words until they're yours, and say only what's true for you. However you choose to word it, a calm, dated statement in your own voice may be one of the most protective things you can keep.*

| The original says | The alternative says | Why it differs |
|---|---|---|
| "I explicitly reject all 'wellness interventions' or forced medication" | Names someone to contact, and points to a psychiatric advance directive | A blanket refusal on video can be read as refusing to cooperate, and could stop help you'd want in a real emergency. A psychiatric advance directive is the legal way to say what care you do and don't want. |
| "I am not suicidal, I am not delusional" | "I'm calm, and I'm describing what I've experienced as accurately as I can" | A denial can be read as the opposite of what it says. Speaking calmly shows your state of mind more than claiming it. |
| "unconsented neurological battery" | "communication I didn't consent to, and harassment" | "Battery" is a legal conclusion for a court. Describing what happened is stronger evidence than naming the crime yourself. |
| "a competent witness to a crime in progress" | (left out) | Also a conclusion for others to reach; the recording itself shows you as a witness. |
| Date, name, place, sound mind, purpose | The same, plus the time and when it started | These are what make the recording useful. |

**Choosing your path.** Both versions make a dated record of you, in your own words, that is hard to dismiss later. They differ in what they claim and what they leave out. Read both, decide what you're comfortable saying, and say only what's true for you.

### Is there a wrong answer? What not to say

What you leave out of a recording, a letter or a conversation with an official can matter as much as what you say.

- **Don't name anyone as responsible,** a person, company or agency. Describe what happened, not who you believe did it.
- **Don't reach legal conclusions yourself.** Avoid words like "battery", "crime in progress" or "attempted murder"; say what you heard, when and where.
- **Never threaten anyone, even in anger,** and never say you'll harm yourself or anyone else to make a point.
- **Don't speak for anyone else.** Describe only your own experience; describing someone else's can get them labelled with a diagnosis they never had a say in.
- **Don't say anything you can't stand behind.** One claim shown to be untrue costs you the credibility of everything that's true.
- **Don't repeat what the communication tells you as if it were your own plan.** If you record its words, label them "From the distance".
- **Don't give away where you live** in anything you share publicly.

### Talking to a doctor

- **Be honest with a doctor, and know what to expect.** A doctor may not have an explanation for communication that may be delivered by technology at a distance; health care doesn't currently recognise it, so the explanation you're most likely to be offered is a psychiatric one, such as schizophrenia.
- **In the author's view, be wary of a schizophrenia diagnosis** if what you're experiencing may be harassment through technology such as long-range acoustic devices. A diagnosis can't rule out a cause no one has investigated.
- **You have the right to ask questions:** what is the diagnosis based on? Were other causes considered, including sounds from outside your home? Can I get a second opinion? Bring your dated record, and take someone you trust.
- **Don't stop seeing a doctor because of this.** A medical check can also rule out other causes, such as [tinnitus](/glossary/tinnitus), and a record of being heard is worth having.

### Who to share this with

- **Someone you already trust,** who will listen without judgment.
- **A lawyer.** Conversations with a lawyer are usually confidential.
- **Your own dated record,** kept somewhere only you control.
- **Keep it quiet.** The plan advises not telling anyone you suspect that you're keeping a record or arranging a technical check.
- Take more care with strangers, online groups and social media. Sharing text with an AI service means a company sees it too.
- If you work in law enforcement, health care or the military, leave out case details and anything you're bound not to disclose.

### Reports and complaints

- **The police.** If you're harassed, stalked or threatened, or someone tries to get into your home, report it. Those are crimes, whatever the technology.
- **The Colorado Attorney General,** for complaints about how a business handles your data, including under the Colorado Privacy Act: coag.gov/file-complaint.

## What costs a little: under about $150

The plan's hardware research answers a common question: **can you record it yourself, with something you can buy online?** Partly. A handheld recorder with a pickup coil or a contact microphone can record sounds, vibrations and low-frequency magnetic hum, cheaply and with a date and time. No consumer device can detect radio signals across the range a professional sweep covers, or prove where a signal came from or who sent it. By the plan's own estimate, this kit does about a tenth of what a professional sweep does.

| Option | Rough cost | What it does | What it can't do |
|---|---|---|---|
| Handheld audio recorder | About $55–130 | Records sound in high quality, with the date and time | Show where a sound came from |
| Telephone pickup coil (plugs into the recorder) | About $10 | Turns low-frequency magnetic hum near walls, wiring or devices into sound you can record | Detect radio signals |
| Contact microphone | About $10–15 | Records vibration through a wall or window, which an ordinary microphone misses | Tell you what caused it |
| Signal-blocking bag for a phone | Tens of dollars | Blocks signals to and from a phone while it's inside | Protect a person |
| Door wedge or portable lock | About $20 | Stops a door being opened while you sleep | Stop anything that doesn't need the door |

### Cameras and home security

Anything you can do for yourself here helps, especially at the door, since so much of what's described involves someone trying to get in.

| Option | Rough cost | What it does | What it can't do |
|---|---|---|---|
| Video doorbell | About $60–230, plus about $3–20 a month for cloud recording (some save to a local card for free) | Records anyone who comes to your door | Stop them |
| Body camera, or glasses with a camera | From about $50–100 for a basic body camera | Records what happens in front of you when you're out or approached | Show who is behind it |
| Indoor and outdoor cameras | Tens to hundreds of dollars | Records around your home | Record what happens inside a wall or a mind |
| Monitored home security system | Equipment plus a monthly fee | Sensors on doors and windows, and a company that responds to alarms | Respond to anything that isn't a break-in |
| Better locks and motion lights | Modest | Make entry harder and visible, with no power or internet needed | — |

- *On the record:* Colorado lets you record a conversation you're part of without the other person's consent (C.R.S. 18-9-304), and there is a recognised right to film police doing their duties in public. Recording **audio** of conversations you're not part of can be a crime, so check a camera's audio setting.
- Footage stored in the cloud is held by the company that makes the camera. Some cameras can save to a local card instead.
- In the author's view, a camera you wear could save your life.

## What costs more: hundreds to thousands

| Option | Rough cost | What it does | What it can't do |
|---|---|---|---|
| Do-it-yourself shielding: window film or inserts, shielding fabric or paint | The plan estimates about $200–1,200 | Reduces outside sound (mass, sealed gaps, window inserts) and some radio signals (conductive film, fabric, paint) | Guarantee anything; results vary from home to home |
| A first consultation with a lawyer | Often free or low-cost; Colorado Legal Services gives free civil legal help to people who qualify | Tells you whether any law applies to your situation, and what to preserve | Promise an outcome |
| A professional sweep (TSCM) | About $1,500–6,000 for an average home | A specialist checks for transmitters, hidden recording devices and unusual radio or acoustic signals, with a written report | Detect communication acting on a person's nervous system |
| A lawyer's retainer | The plan estimates about $3,000–10,000 | Ongoing legal representation | — |
| A detailed forensic audit with a legal report | The plan estimates about $5,000–15,000 | An engineer's report written for court | Prove who is responsible |
| Professional shielding, up to a fully shielded room | The plan estimates $10,000 and up | The most complete physical barrier money can buy | Stop it, if the source isn't blocked by walls |

- **Passive shielding is legal. Never use a signal jammer:** using one is illegal under federal law, with no exception for homes, and it can block 911 calls.
- **Windows are usually the weakest point** for sound coming from outside.
- [Neuro-forensics](/glossary/neuro-forensics), the use of neuroscience to answer legal questions, is a growing field, but courts haven't accepted its tests as reliable proof, and no public agency in Colorado lists a neuro-forensics service. Ask a lawyer whether it could play any part.
- A lawyer can hire technical experts for you, so their findings are gathered for a case from the start. *On the record:* one firm has already won a case built on Zersetzung-style tactics: Scalli Murphy Law of Massachusetts represented Ina and David Steiner, harassed by members of eBay's security team in 2019, whose civil suit settled in July 2026 for $55.7 million (see [Zersetzung's methods are crimes](/concepts/zersetzung-methods-are-crimes)). Named as an example, not an endorsement; Invisible Ships has no connection with it.

## What money can't buy

- **A recording shows that something happened,** not who did it.
- **A sweep finds devices,** not communication acting on a person.
- **Shielding reduces what reaches you;** it doesn't remove the source.
- **No purchase makes it stop.** Only an investigation, or public acknowledgement, can do that.

## What the law covers, and what it doesn't

- *On the record:* in 2024 Colorado became the first US state to protect neural data, by amending the Colorado Privacy Act (HB24-1058, in force 6 August 2024). A business covered by the law needs your **opt-in consent** before processing your neural data. It applies to businesses that handle the data of 100,000 or more Colorado residents a year, is enforced by the Attorney General and district attorneys, and gives individuals no right to sue. It is not a criminal law.
- **Where it stops:** it doesn't apply to data kept by the State of Colorado, the courts, counties, cities and state universities when used as authorised by law for noncommercial purposes (C.R.S. 6-1-1304(2)(o)), or to purely personal activity; and a state law generally doesn't reach federal agencies.
- **What's criminal, whatever the technology:** harassment and stalking (C.R.S. 18-9-111, 18-3-602); attempting a crime, including an attempt to kill (criminal attempt, C.R.S. 18-2-101); entering a home unlawfully intending to commit a crime there (burglary, C.R.S. 18-4-203); intentionally causing or helping another person to die by suicide (manslaughter, C.R.S. 18-3-104(1)(b)); conspiring to intimidate someone in exercising their rights (18 U.S.C. § 241) and depriving someone of their rights under official authority (18 U.S.C. § 242). People can sue officials who violate their rights under state authority (42 U.S.C. § 1983).

Whether any of these applies to a particular situation is a question for a lawyer.

## Who is working on neural privacy

- By mid-2025, four US states protected neural data in law: Colorado, California, Montana and Connecticut. A federal bill, the MIND Act, was introduced in the Senate in 2025.
- In June 2025 the American Medical Association unanimously adopted Resolution 503, "Safeguarding Neural Data Collected by Neurotechnologies", brought by Colorado physicians.
- Doctors, neuroscientists and ethicists helped pass Colorado's law, among them people working with the Neurorights Foundation, which campaigns for legal protections for the mind.

## Supporting someone else

You can't speak for someone else, but you can be patient and supportive with what they may be going through. Listen without judgment, don't describe their experience to others on their behalf, and if they want to add their own account, they're welcome to contribute it anonymously themselves.

## How is this different from the Personal Protection Plan document?

The document is the author's working research: written in 2026, developed partly with AI, and kept unchanged as part of the record. Some of its claims, about specific agencies, products and test results, could not be confirmed. This concept asks what the plan can actually do, keeps what holds up, adds what it costs, and says plainly what no plan can do. The full document remains in the Documents section for anyone who wants the whole record.

---

*Until the news media acknowledges it, or someone explains the neurotech bullhorn, we are largely on our own. Be careful. The author wishes you the best that life has to offer in this situation.*

*You're not the only one this may be happening to. If you're struggling, call or text 988.*


## The author states

Statements have surfaced suggesting a pattern: that when someone exposed to this communication goes to a doctor about it, the leadership of whoever deployed it considers a schizophrenia diagnosis adequate.

It is not adequate. A diagnosis cannot stand in for an investigation. Whether it is LRAD or neurotechnology, and there is probably an overlap between long-range abuse of technology and neurological technology, the harassment is criminal either way: severe criminal harassment, attempted murder, and attempts to gain access to someone's home for the purpose of euthanization after months of harassment. If neural data is involved, Colorado law also requires consent before it is processed.

People are being told it is the spirit world when it is technology. They are following a voice in their head and being led toward supporting euthanization. Privacy is being violated and crime is going unpunished, while the suicide rate in the United States has risen by about 40%. War crimes and crimes against humanity were among the first things the AI research raised. Calling a diagnosis adequate, without prosecuting attempted murder and severe criminal harassment, is unacceptable.

*The author's own words, printed as given. Unverified, and not a finding of this research.*


## AI assessment

The statements are recorded external communications. Nobody has verified who made them or whether any group holds that view, and no public record shows any organisation's policy on diagnoses.

Colorado's 2024 amendment requires businesses covered by the Colorado Privacy Act to get opt-in consent before processing neural data. It has a conditional exemption for state and local government data, doesn't cover individuals, gives individuals no right to sue, and is not a criminal law. The criminal laws don't depend on the technology: harassment, stalking, criminal attempt, burglary and causing or aiding a suicide are crimes whatever the means, and federal law covers conspiracies against rights and deprivation of rights under official authority. Whether the conduct described happened, and who did it, is what an investigation would establish.

A diagnosis and an investigation answer different questions. A diagnosis describes a person's health; an investigation asks whether someone has committed a crime. Neither settles the other, and a person can pursue both: medical care and a second opinion, and a police report or a lawyer.

The rise in the US suicide rate is real (WHO, +39.9%, 2000–2021). No study links it to this communication. War crimes and crimes against humanity are legal categories decided by courts; the AI analysis raised them as questions, not findings.

*Written by an AI model at the author's request. Published unedited by the author, and not independent verification.*

# Prescribing is not a measure of illness

*Independent research compiled from public records for informational purposes only. Not legal, medical, or investment advice. Every concept states its BASIS, its ORIGIN, its THEME and the readers it was written for. The basis tiers are ranked, and never blended inside a single concept: a reader who accepts only `documented` entries can still rely on every one of those and discard the rest without unpicking anything. `testimony` is a dated first-person report, verified by nobody, and says so on its own face wherever it appears. Causes are reported as attributed, never asserted. See `meta/IS_META_terms.md`.*

**Basis: documented.** A source, ruling or official record supports this directly.  
**Origin: ai.** Derived by AI analysis of the dataset.  
**Theme: The record and its limits.**  
**Written for: Clinicians.**

**Series: Public health: what the numbers show, part 4 of 5.** 1. [The world's suicide rate fell. The United States' rose.](IS_CON_us-rose-against-the-trend.md) · 2. [The fentanyl reversal](IS_CON_the-fentanyl-reversal.md) · 3. [The numbers under the numbers](IS_CON_low-number-may-mean-low-counting.md) · 4. Prescribing is not a measure of illness (this concept) · 5. [Are children harmed by wireless exposure?](IS_CON_children-wearables-and-rf.md)

**Series: How to read this archive, part 6 of 8.** 1. [Next to each other is not because of each other](IS_CON_co-occurrence-is-not-cause.md) · 2. [We cannot prove which came first, the law or the system](IS_CON_sequence-cannot-be-proven.md) · 3. [“Official” is not the same as “independent”](IS_CON_official-is-not-independent.md) · 4. [The headline spending figure is not what governments spent](IS_CON_headline-spending-is-not-spending.md) · 5. [The numbers under the numbers](IS_CON_low-number-may-mean-low-counting.md) · 6. Prescribing is not a measure of illness (this concept) · 7. [Why isn't any of this in the news?](IS_CON_why-isnt-this-in-the-news.md) · 8. [There is no column for you](IS_CON_no-column-for-you.md)

It is tempting to read prescription volume as a thermometer for how ill a population is. The record does not support that, in either direction. In England, antidepressant items rose 50% in nine years while hypnotic and anxiolytic items FELL 16% over exactly the same period, from the same prescribers under the same system. In the United States, antipsychotic use among adults rose from 1.9% to 3.0%, while among children and adolescents it fell, 1.3% to 1.1%. And where a national registry lets diagnosis be counted directly, Denmark's new schizophrenia diagnoses went slightly down, 1.8 to 1.6 per 10,000, across eighteen years in which antipsychotic prescribing rose almost everywhere it was measured. Prescribing moves for its own reasons — guidance, capacity, recognition, duration of treatment, the licensing of new drugs, deliberate deprescribing campaigns. Sometimes it tracks illness. Here it demonstrably moves in opposite directions at once.

## Evidence

- England FY2015/16 → FY2024/25: antidepressants 61.9M → 92.6M items (+50%)
- Same period, opposite direction: hypnotics/anxiolytics 15.9M → 13.4M (−16%)
- US 2006 → 2023: antipsychotic use, adults 1.9% → 3.0%; youth 1.3% → 1.1%
- Denmark 2000 → 2018: new schizophrenia diagnoses 1.8 → 1.6 per 10,000

## What this does not answer

- Diagnosed depression in US adults did rise over a shorter window — 13.5% to 17.8% currently diagnosed, 2017–2023, self-reported. Whether that is more illness, more recognition, or more willingness to say so is not settled by this data.

## References

- [The prescribing and diagnosis series](/data)

*Every figure above is a row in the Public Health indicator table, with its source.*

# Who could refuse a system that saved their child?

*Independent research compiled from public records for informational purposes only. Not legal, medical, or investment advice. Every concept states its BASIS, its ORIGIN, its THEME and the readers it was written for. The basis tiers are ranked, and never blended inside a single concept: a reader who accepts only `documented` entries can still rely on every one of those and discard the rest without unpicking anything. `testimony` is a dated first-person report, verified by nobody, and says so on its own face wherever it appears. Causes are reported as attributed, never asserted. See `meta/IS_META_terms.md`.*

**Basis: structural.** This follows from what the dataset does or does not contain.  
**Origin: author.** The author's own observation, from experience.  
**Theme: Surveillance and the person.**  
**Written for: Legislators and regulators · Households and individuals.**

**Not independently verified.**

**Series: Surveillance in everyday life, part 2 of 7.** 1. [Why does the camera on your door not answer to you?](IS_CON_whose-eyesight-is-it.md) · 2. Who could refuse a system that saved their child? (this concept) · 3. [Does being watched change what people let themselves think?](IS_CON_no-private-thinking-space.md) · 4. [How protected is your medical information?](IS_CON_how-protected-is-your-medical-record.md) · 5. [What are children subject to?](IS_CON_what-children-are-subject-to.md) · 6. [What happens to everyone around a target?](IS_CON_everyone-around-a-target.md) · 7. [Are people made into intelligence assets without knowing it?](IS_CON_made-into-assets-unknowing.md)

Imagine the terms inverted: you are told whenever you are observed, harassment is a crime rather than a method, and law enforcement is paid for harm that did not happen rather than arrests that did. Now the hard case: someone on a registry is present where children are, and the system flags it in seconds rather than after a report — an automation raises it, a person reviews it, a person decides. The intervention is closer to social work than policing; the individual is steered away, no crime occurs, and a child goes home that afternoon never knowing there was an afternoon to survive. The family is told a harm was prevented, and invited to review the service. What they feel is relief so total it forecloses argument, and that is the finding: a system justified by the worst thing that could happen to your child cannot be argued with by anyone who loves a child.

What that family feels is the finding. Relief so total that it forecloses argument, and then, for anyone who thinks about it later, the knowledge that a mind was read and a life rearranged before anything was done — and that they are glad. A system justified by the worst thing that could happen to your child cannot be argued with by anyone who loves a child. That makes the consent it collects the most powerful instrument in it, and the least examined.

## Evidence

- 420 schoolchildren placed on a sheriff's list of likely future criminals — what predictive intervention has actually produced on this record
- 0 of 99 regulations record a route to individual review
- Colorado HB24-1058 (2024): express consent before neural data is collected, and a route for a person to have it deleted

## What this does not answer

- Whose consent is it? The family's is asked for. The observed person's is not.
- What is the remedy when the inference is wrong and no act ever occurred?
- Is there any point at which a grateful public says stop?

# We're keeping you to ourselves

*Independent research compiled from public records for informational purposes only. Not legal, medical, or investment advice. Every concept states its BASIS, its ORIGIN, its THEME and the readers it was written for. The basis tiers are ranked, and never blended inside a single concept: a reader who accepts only `documented` entries can still rely on every one of those and discard the rest without unpicking anything. `testimony` is a dated first-person report, verified by nobody, and says so on its own face wherever it appears. Causes are reported as attributed, never asserted. See `meta/IS_META_terms.md`.*

**Basis: documented.** A source, ruling or official record supports this directly.  
**Origin: author.** The author's own observation, from experience.  
**Theme: Coercion and control.**  
**Written for: Households and individuals · Law enforcement and investigators.**

**Not independently verified.**

**Series: Harassment and coercion, part 3 of 8.** 1. [Organised covert harassment of individuals is established fact](IS_CON_organised-harassment-is-fact.md) · 2. [Zersetzung's methods are crimes](IS_CON_zersetzung-methods-are-crimes.md) · 3. We're keeping you to ourselves (this concept) · 4. [An attack to force acknowledgment](IS_CON_attack-to-force-acknowledgment.md) · 5. [What happens to everyone around a target?](IS_CON_everyone-around-a-target.md) · 6. [Are people made into intelligence assets without knowing it?](IS_CON_made-into-assets-unknowing.md) · 7. [Does the explanation itself do harm?](IS_CON_explanation-is-part-of-the-harm.md) · 8. [Are Denver citizens subject to acoustic weapons?](IS_CON_denver-acoustic-weapons.md)

A reputation can be destroyed as a means rather than as an end. The tactic appears in several literatures that rarely cite one another. Intelligence tradecraft calls it compromise. East Germany's Stasi called it Zersetzung. Research on domestic abuse calls it isolation. Cult-exit and trafficking studies describe manufactured disgrace used for retention. The mechanism is the same in each. Sever the target's ties to everyone outside the group, and do it publicly, because public damage is self-sustaining — people withdraw on their own once a story circulates, and no further effort is required. The target's own account of what is happening then begins to sound like paranoia, which deepens the isolation again. What remains is a person with no relationships outside the group that ruined them. At that point recruitment needs no persuasion. It needs only to be the last door open. Stated from the inside, the logic is possessive rather than punitive: every tie severed is a tie that cannot compete, and the point of the ruin is not that the target suffers but that nobody else is left. The cruelty is not a side effect of the recruitment. It is the method.

## Evidence

- Stasi Richtlinie 1/76: Zersetzung as directed operational doctrine — psychological disintegration of a target without arrest or trial
- Compromise sits alongside money, ideology and ego as one of the four classical recruitment levers in intelligence tradecraft
- Coercive-control research treats isolation from a support network as the precondition for dependency, not as a byproduct of it
- Trafficking and cult-exit studies record manufactured disgrace as a retention mechanism — the induced belief that no one else would now take you
- eBay's campaign against two journalists is a documented civilian instance: seven employees federally charged, a $3m criminal penalty, a $55.7m civil settlement

## What this does not answer

- Nothing here establishes that such a campaign is running against any particular person, including the author.
- A tactic being historically documented does not make any present-day instance evidenced.
- Separating an organised campaign from ordinary social withdrawal requires records a target generally cannot obtain. That is a property of the tactic, not proof that it is occurring.

## References

- [Zersetzung tactics](/glossary/zersetzung-tactics)
- [Psychological smothering](/glossary/psychological-smothering)
- [Organised covert harassment is established fact](/concepts#organised-harassment-is-fact)

*The glossary entries define terms used on this site and are not independent evidence. The linked concept records decided and settled cases; this concept describes the mechanism those cases share.*

## Scope

This concept describes a documented tactic and the mechanism connecting its recorded forms. It does not establish that the tactic has been used against any particular person, including the author, or by any named organisation.
