# International Criminal Court (ICC)

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


The world's first permanent international criminal court, based in The Hague. It tries individuals, not states, for genocide, war crimes, crimes against humanity and the crime of aggression.

It was created by the Rome Statute, a treaty adopted in Rome on 17 July 1998 that entered into force on 1 July 2002. It is a court of last resort: it complements national courts rather than replacing them, and acts when a country is unwilling or unable to investigate or prosecute.

Related terms: [tribunal](/glossary/tribunal), [Nuremberg Code](/glossary/nuremberg-code), [no-touch torture](/glossary/no-touch-torture)

Sources: [The Court — International Criminal Court](https://www.icc-cpi.int/about/the-court) · [Rome Statute of the International Criminal Court: status and entry into force — United Nations Treaty Collection](https://treaties.un.org/doc/publication/mtdsg/volume%20ii/chapter%20xviii/xviii-10.en.pdf)

# laser microphone

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


Reading speech off a window from outside it. Sound inside a room vibrates the glass by a few billionths of a metre; a laser or radar beam reflected off the pane carries those vibrations back, and the speech is reconstructed from them. No device is placed in the room and nothing is installed.

This is the reverse of [structure-borne audio](/glossary/structure-borne-audio). There, a transducer drives a window so the glass speaks. Here, a beam reads a window so the glass listens. The same pane serves both, which is why it appears in accounts from both directions.

Documented real-world example: a 2026 survey in Sensors reports a millimetre-wave system recovering spoken digits at better than 93 per cent accuracy through soundproof glass at five metres, and LiDAR bounced off ordinary household objects reaching 91 per cent at 1.5 metres. Large-vocabulary speech recognition from vibration reached word error rates of 9 to 15 per cent at half a metre to 1.5 metres.

It matters for assessing a claim because it is genuinely remote — unlike every documented way of making a window produce sound, which requires physically touching it. A room can be listened to from outside without anyone entering. It cannot, on the available evidence, be spoken into the same way.

Related terms: [structure-borne audio](/glossary/structure-borne-audio), [contact microphone](/glossary/contact-microphone), [piezoelectricity](/glossary/piezoelectricity), [image-based search](/glossary/image-based-search)

Sources: [Speech recovery from vibration: laser, radar and LiDAR sensing — Sensors 26(8):2553](https://www.mdpi.com/1424-8220/26/8/2553); [Protection of speech information against laser eavesdropping — Journal of Vibroengineering (2016)](https://www.extrica.com/article/16943)

# lipid nanoparticle (LNP)

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


A tiny sphere made of fats (lipids) that carries fragile genetic material, such as mRNA, into cells. Without it, mRNA would break down in the body before it could be used.

The Pfizer-BioNTech and Moderna COVID-19 vaccines each use a lipid nanoparticle made of four lipids: an ionisable lipid (ALC-0315 in Pfizer, SM-102 in Moderna), a helper lipid (DSPC), cholesterol, and a PEG-lipid. The mixture assembles around the mRNA into a particle about the size of a virus, is taken up by the cell, and releases the mRNA inside. The ingredient lists are published by regulators, including the FDA, Health Canada and the European Medicines Agency.

A lipid nanoparticle is a chemical package. It contains no electronics, sensors or antennas, and it is not a device.

On the record: claims that COVID-19 vaccines contain graphene oxide or other graphene materials have been checked repeatedly by fact-checkers including Reuters, AFP and PolitiFact, and found false. Graphene oxide is not on any published ingredient list, and Pfizer has stated it is not used in making its vaccine.

Related terms: [nanoparticle](/glossary/nanoparticle), [nanomedicine](/glossary/nanomedicine), [mRNA](/glossary/mrna), [graphene](/glossary/graphene)

Sources: [Components of COVID-19 vaccines — Health Canada](https://www.canada.ca/en/public-health/services/diseases/2019-novel-coronavirus-infection/awareness-resources/components-covid-19-vaccines.html); [LNP carriers of the COVID-19 mRNA vaccines — Pharmaceutics (2021)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8707022/); [COVID-19 vaccines do not contain graphene — PolitiFact (2022)](https://politifact.com/factchecks/2022/jan/24/facebook-posts/covid-19-vaccines-do-not-contain-graphene-are-not/); [Does the Pfizer vaccine contain graphene oxide? — Check Your Fact (2021)](https://checkyourfact.com/2021/07/12/fact-check-pfizer-covid-vaccine-graphene-oxide/)

# long-range acoustic device (LRAD)

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


A loudspeaker system that projects sound, including clear speech, in a narrow beam over long distances, so that people in its path hear it clearly while people beside it hear far less. LRAD is the brand name of devices made by Genasys (formerly LRAD Corporation) of San Diego, and is often used for the whole category.

Navies and ships use them to hail and warn other vessels, a use that grew after the attack on the USS Cole in 2000. Police use them for announcements and crowd control; the first widely reported use against protesters in the United States was at the 2009 G20 summit in Pittsburgh. At high volume the alert tone can cause pain and hearing damage, and in 2018 a federal appeals court held that police use of the device against protesters can amount to excessive force (Edrei v. Maguire).

Related terms: [parametric array](/glossary/parametric-array), [Mosquito device](/glossary/mosquito-device), [structure-borne audio](/glossary/structure-borne-audio), [directed energy](/glossary/directed-energy)

# magnetoelectric nanoparticles (MENPs)

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


mag·NEE·toh·ee·LEK·trik

Nanoparticles, typically a magnetic core inside a shell of another material, that produce a small local electric field when a magnetic field is applied to them. Because neurons work on electrical signals, researchers are studying them as a way to stimulate, and possibly record, brain activity without surgery or wires.

The work is at the laboratory and animal stage. Experiments have stimulated and, in a 2024 cell-culture study, also suppressed neural activity, and earlier mouse studies used a magnet to draw injected particles into the brain.

Documented real-world example: magnetoelectric nanoparticles were part of DARPA’s Next-Generation Nonsurgical Neurotechnology (N3) programme, which sought two-way brain–machine interfaces for able-bodied service members. A Battelle-led project called BrainSTORMS proposed injecting magnetoelectric nanotransducers and communicating with them through a helmet-based transceiver.

Related terms: [nanoparticle](/glossary/nanoparticle), [electromagnetic field](/glossary/electromagnetic-field), [blood–brain barrier](/glossary/blood-brain-barrier), [brain–computer interface](/glossary/braincomputer-interface-bci)

Sources: [Battelle to develop injectable bi-directional brain-computer interface — Medical Design Briefs](https://www.medicaldesignbriefs.com/component/content/article/34496-battelle-to-develop-injectable-bi-directional-brain-computer-interface); [Controlling action potentials with magnetoelectric nanoparticles — Brain Stimulation (2024)](https://doaj.org/article/801d9135b2074d888babee5afd7992cc)

# microwave auditory effect

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


also “Frey effect”

A documented phenomenon in which pulsed microwave or radio-frequency energy is perceived as clicks, buzzes, or hums that seem to originate inside the head, with no external sound. First reported by scientist Allan H. Frey in 1961, it arises through thermoelastic expansion — the pulsed energy causes rapid, minute heating of head tissue, generating a tiny pressure wave that the inner ear detects as sound. It is the real, established basis most often cited in discussions of delivering sound to a person without a speaker.

Related terms: [Voice-to-skull](/glossary/voice-to-skull), [Directed-energy](/glossary/directed-energy), [telepathy](/glossary/telepathy)

Sources: [Microwave auditory effect — Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microwave_auditory_effect)

# military ugly workflow

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


A name that has surfaced in statements recorded in the journal, from 2025 onwards, for a strategy of luring a person out of their home, or getting them to let someone in. In the statements, the person is offered something they want, such as money, intimacy, drugs, relief from suffering, a job or a family, and then asked to open their door, or to go outside to a meeting place, for example by walking three blocks west. The statements link it to pressure toward euthanization.

The name and what it describes come only from recorded statements, which are external communications and unverified. No public record of a program by this name has been found.

Related terms: [Zersetzung tactics](/glossary/zersetzung-tactics), [gaslighting](/glossary/gaslighting), [euthanasia outside legitimate health care](/glossary/euthanasia-outside-health-care)

Sources: the Invisible Ships journal (about 150 recordings mention "ugly workflows", from August 2025); [Do Not Choose Yourself](/concepts/do-not-choose-yourself).

# the Mosquito

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


A commercial anti-loitering device that emits a continuous high-frequency tone, around 17.4 kHz, loud enough to be unpleasant and pitched high enough that older adults are less likely to hear it. Invented by Howard Stapleton and first trialled at a shop in Barry, Wales, in November 2005. Sold with a stated range of 50 to 70 feet at 85 dB, 100 dB maximum. It won an Ig Nobel Peace Prize in 2006.

It belongs in this glossary for one reason. A sound that some people hear and others standing beside them do not requires no exotic technology whatsoever. It is a purchasable product, legal in most jurisdictions, and it has been deployed on public streets in the United Kingdom, Canada, Australia, Belgium, France and Ireland.

The vendor’s age claim should be read carefully. Marketing describes it as affecting roughly ages 13 to 25, but hearing loss at these frequencies is a gradual slope rather than a cutoff — see [presbycusis](/glossary/presbycusis).

Documented real-world example: in 2010 the Parliamentary Assembly of the Council of Europe adopted Recommendation 1930, calling on governments to ban the device and citing Articles 3, 8 and 11 of the European Convention on Human Rights and Articles 2, 3, 15, 19 and 31 of the UN Convention on the Rights of the Child. In 2016 the UN Committee on the Rights of the Child explicitly called on the United Kingdom to ban its use; the Home Office declined. A French court at Saint-Brieuc prohibited local use in 2008 and awarded damages, and Sheffield City Council barred it from council property in 2011. No national statutory ban has been enacted anywhere. In 2006 schoolchildren repurposed the tone as the “Teen Buzz” ringtone, inaudible to their teachers.

Related terms: [presbycusis](/glossary/presbycusis), [parametric array](/glossary/parametric-array), [no-touch torture](/glossary/no-touch-torture), [psychological smothering](/glossary/psychological-smothering)

Sources: [The Mosquito — Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Mosquito); [Recommendation 1978 (2011), citing Recommendation 1930 — Parliamentary Assembly of the Council of Europe](https://www.assembly.coe.int/nw/xml/XRef/Xref-XML2HTML-en.asp?fileid=18019&lang=en); [Mosquito devices — Children & Young People’s Commissioner Scotland](https://www.cypcs.org.uk/positions/mosquito-devices/); [Home Office rejects call for ban — BBC News](https://www.bbc.co.uk/news/10429722)

# mRNA (messenger RNA)

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


A molecule that carries instructions from a cell’s DNA to the machinery that builds proteins.

An mRNA vaccine delivers instructions for one protein from a virus (for COVID-19, the spike protein). The cell builds that protein for a short time, and the immune system learns to recognise it. The mRNA does not enter the cell nucleus, where DNA is kept, and it breaks down after use.

Because mRNA is fragile, it has to be packaged for delivery. In the COVID-19 vaccines, that package is a [lipid nanoparticle](/glossary/lipid-nanoparticle).

Related terms: [lipid nanoparticle](/glossary/lipid-nanoparticle), [nanomedicine](/glossary/nanomedicine)

Sources: [What are the ingredients in the COVID-19 mRNA vaccines? — Vaccine Education Center, Children’s Hospital of Philadelphia](https://www.chop.edu/centers-programs/vaccine-education-center/video/what-are-the-different-ingredients-being-used-in-the-covid-19-mrna-vaccines)

# muon tomography

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


also muon imaging, “muography”

An imaging technique that uses muons — high-energy subatomic particles created when cosmic rays strike the upper atmosphere (and also producible with particle accelerators) — to see inside large or dense objects that ordinary imaging can't penetrate. Muons pass through great thicknesses of matter, but are absorbed and deflected at rates that depend on density, so detectors on the far side can reconstruct a “shadow” image of what lies within. It has been used to map the interiors of volcanoes, inspect nuclear reactors (including Fukushima), scan cargo for shielded nuclear material, and reveal hidden voids in the Great Pyramid of Giza and beneath ancient Jerusalem.

Related terms: [non-ionizing radiation](/glossary/non-ionizing-radiation), [hardware](/glossary/hardware-computer), [Directed-energy](/glossary/directed-energy)

Sources: [Muon imaging at an ancient Jerusalem site — AIP, Journal of Applied Physics](https://pubs.aip.org/aip/jap/article/138/8/084504/3361099/First-demonstration-of-underground-muon-imaging-at); [Directional muon beam for imaging — Lawrence Berkeley National Lab](https://atap.lbl.gov/news/directional-muon-beam-shows-potential-for-advanced-imaging-technique/)

# nanomaterial

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


Any material with at least one dimension in the nanoscale range, roughly 1 to 100 nanometres. Examples include [graphene](/glossary/graphene), which is one atom thick; carbon nanotubes; nanowires; and [nanoparticles](/glossary/nanoparticle).

In neurotechnology, nanomaterials are used to make the parts of a device that touch the brain thinner, more flexible and closer to the softness of tissue, and in some cases transparent. The aim is a device the body tolerates for longer and that records more cleanly.

Documented real-world example: the [transparent brain-computer interfaces](/glossary/transparent-brain-computer-interfaces-bcis) built at UC San Diego use a graphene electrode array thin and clear enough for a microscope to see through it.

Related terms: [nanotechnology](/glossary/nanotechnology), [graphene](/glossary/graphene), [injectable mesh electronics](/glossary/injectable-mesh-electronics), [transparent brain-computer interfaces](/glossary/transparent-brain-computer-interfaces-bcis)

Sources: [Just How Small Is Nano? — U.S. National Nanotechnology Initiative](https://www.nano.gov/about-nanotechnology/just-how-small-is-nano); [Transparent Brain-Computer Interface Uses AI and Nanotech — Psychology Today (Cami Rosso, 24 Jan 2024)](https://www.psychologytoday.com/us/blog/the-future-brain/202401/transparent-brain-computer-interface-uses-ai-and-nanotech)

# nanomedicine

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


The use of nanotechnology to prevent, diagnose or treat disease.

Most of the field works on delivery: getting a medicine or genetic instruction to the right cells, in the right amount, without it breaking down on the way. Other areas include imaging agents that make diseased tissue easier to see, and treatments designed to cross the [blood–brain barrier](/glossary/blood-brain-barrier), which blocks most medicines from reaching the brain.

Documented real-world example: the [lipid nanoparticle](/glossary/lipid-nanoparticle) in the mRNA COVID-19 vaccines is the most widely used nanomedicine to date, given in billions of doses.

Related terms: [nanotechnology](/glossary/nanotechnology), [nanoparticle](/glossary/nanoparticle), [lipid nanoparticle](/glossary/lipid-nanoparticle), [blood–brain barrier](/glossary/blood-brain-barrier)

Sources: [Lipid Nanoparticles, Key Component of COVID-19 mRNA Vaccines — StatNano](https://statnano.com/news/69392/Lipid-Nanoparticles-Key-Component-of-COVID-19-mRNA-Vaccines)

# nanoparticle

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


A particle roughly 1 to 100 nanometres across. Nanoparticles can be made from fats, metals, polymers or ceramics, depending on the job.

In medicine they are mostly used as carriers: they package a drug or genetic material so it survives the trip through the body and reaches the right cells. In neuroscience research they are also being studied as a way to stimulate brain cells from outside the head, using magnetic fields or light that passes through the skull.

Documented real-world example: the mRNA COVID-19 vaccines from Pfizer-BioNTech and Moderna each use a [lipid nanoparticle](/glossary/lipid-nanoparticle) to carry the mRNA into cells.

Related terms: [nanotechnology](/glossary/nanotechnology), [lipid nanoparticle](/glossary/lipid-nanoparticle), [magnetoelectric nanoparticles](/glossary/magnetoelectric-nanoparticles), [optogenetics](/glossary/optogenetics), [blood–brain barrier](/glossary/blood-brain-barrier)

Sources: [Just How Small Is Nano? — U.S. National Nanotechnology Initiative](https://www.nano.gov/about-nanotechnology/just-how-small-is-nano); [Components of COVID-19 vaccines — Health Canada](https://www.canada.ca/en/public-health/services/diseases/2019-novel-coronavirus-infection/awareness-resources/components-covid-19-vaccines.html)

# nanotechnology

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


The science and engineering of materials and devices at the scale of roughly 1 to 100 nanometres. A nanometre is one billionth of a metre. A sheet of paper is about 100,000 nanometres thick.

At this scale materials can behave differently from the same material in bulk: stronger, more conductive, more reactive, or see-through. That is why nanotechnology turns up in electronics, medicine and neuroscience alike.

In medicine, its most widely used product so far is the [lipid nanoparticle](/glossary/lipid-nanoparticle) that carries mRNA in the Pfizer-BioNTech and Moderna COVID-19 vaccines. In neurotechnology, it is used to make electrodes thinner, softer, wireless or transparent, so that they disturb brain tissue less. The best-known example on this site is the [transparent brain-computer interfaces](/glossary/transparent-brain-computer-interfaces-bcis).

Related terms: [nanomaterial](/glossary/nanomaterial), [nanoparticle](/glossary/nanoparticle), [nanomedicine](/glossary/nanomedicine), [neural interface](/glossary/neural-interface), [transparent brain-computer interfaces](/glossary/transparent-brain-computer-interfaces-bcis)

Sources: [Just How Small Is Nano? — U.S. National Nanotechnology Initiative](https://www.nano.gov/about-nanotechnology/just-how-small-is-nano); [Transparent Brain-Computer Interface Uses AI and Nanotech — Psychology Today (Cami Rosso, 24 Jan 2024)](https://www.psychologytoday.com/us/blog/the-future-brain/202401/transparent-brain-computer-interface-uses-ai-and-nanotech)

# neural decoding

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


Using software, increasingly machine learning, to turn recorded brain signals into information: an intended movement, a word, an image, or an estimate of activity the device cannot directly measure.

Decoding is what makes a [brain–computer interface](/glossary/braincomputer-interface-bci) useful. It is also where the questions of [cognitive liberty](/glossary/cognitive-liberty) begin, because the better the decoding, the more a recording can reveal.

Documented real-world example: in the UC San Diego study behind [transparent brain-computer interfaces](/glossary/transparent-brain-computer-interfaces-bcis), a neural network called a BiLSTM (bidirectional long short-term memory), which is built to handle signals that change over time, learned to predict deep [calcium imaging](/glossary/calcium-imaging) activity in mice from surface electrical recordings alone. The authors reported it could predict both average activity and the activity of single cells.

Related terms: [artificial intelligence](/glossary/artificial-intelligence), [software](/glossary/software), [brain–computer interface](/glossary/braincomputer-interface-bci), [calcium imaging](/glossary/calcium-imaging), [cognitive liberty](/glossary/cognitive-liberty)

Sources: [Neural decoding — Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neural_decoding); [Transparent Brain-Computer Interface Uses AI and Nanotech — Psychology Today (Cami Rosso, 24 Jan 2024)](https://www.psychologytoday.com/us/blog/the-future-brain/202401/transparent-brain-computer-interface-uses-ai-and-nanotech)

# neural dust

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


Tiny wireless, battery-free sensors that are placed inside the body and powered and read using ultrasound from outside it. Developed at UC Berkeley and first shown in rats in 2016.

Each 2016 sensor, or mote, was about 3 millimetres long and 1 millimetre across, implanted on a nerve or muscle. It holds a piezoelectric crystal and a simple circuit. An ultrasound transducer outside the body sends pulses; the mote reflects them back slightly changed by the electrical activity it is touching, and that change is read as the signal. Ultrasound was chosen over radio because it travels through tissue far better at this size. A 2018 version, StimDust, can also stimulate nerves.

The early work was on nerves and muscles. The inventors describe the brain as a future target, with further shrinking of the motes.

Related terms: [neural interface](/glossary/neural-interface), [hardware](/glossary/hardware-computer), [piezoelectricity](/glossary/piezoelectricity), [injectable mesh electronics](/glossary/injectable-mesh-electronics)

Sources: [Sprinkling of neural dust opens door to electroceuticals — Berkeley News (2016)](https://news.berkeley.edu/2016/08/03/sprinkling-of-neural-dust-opens-door-to-electroceuticals/); [Berkeley engineers build smallest volume, most efficient wireless nerve stimulator — Berkeley News (2018)](https://news.berkeley.edu/2018/04/10/berkeley-engineers-build-smallest-volume-most-efficient-wireless-nerve-stimulator)

# neural interface

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


Any device that records signals from the nervous system, sends signals into it, or both. A [brain–computer interface](/glossary/braincomputer-interface-bci) is one kind: a neural interface connected to a computer.

Neural interfaces range from caps worn on the scalp, such as the [electroencephalogram](/glossary/electroencephalogram), to sheets of electrodes placed on the brain’s surface ([electrocorticography](/glossary/electrocorticography)) and implants that go into brain tissue. The closer a device sits to the neurons, the clearer the signal, and the more surgery it needs.

Much current research, including nanotechnology, aims to break that trade-off: to get a clear signal with a device that is smaller, softer, wireless or less invasive.

Related terms: [brain–computer interface](/glossary/braincomputer-interface-bci), [hardware](/glossary/hardware-computer), [electrocorticography](/glossary/electrocorticography), [neural dust](/glossary/neural-dust), [injectable mesh electronics](/glossary/injectable-mesh-electronics), [neural decoding](/glossary/neural-decoding)

Sources: [Brain–computer interface — Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brain%E2%80%93computer_interface)

# neural privacy

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


The right to keep the activity of your brain and nervous system, and anything that can be read from it, private, and to decide who may collect, use or share it.

In the United States, Colorado became the first state to protect neural data by law in 2024, followed by California the same year and by Montana and Connecticut in 2025; each treats neural data as sensitive and gives people control over how companies handle it. Chile was the first country to protect mental integrity and brain activity in its constitution, in 2021, and in 2023 its Supreme Court ordered a company to delete a user's brain data. In November 2025, UNESCO's member states adopted the first global standard on the ethics of neurotechnology, which treats neural data as uniquely sensitive.

These laws cover companies and data. None yet addresses communication directed at a person's mind without their consent.

Related terms: [Colorado Privacy Act](/glossary/colorado-privacy-act), [cognitive liberty](/glossary/cognitive-liberty), [neuroethics](/glossary/neuroethics), [neurotechnology](/glossary/neurotechnology)

Sources: [Your brain, their rules: the growing patchwork of neural data regulation — Cooley (2026)](https://www.cooley.com/news/insight/2026/2026-02-23-your-brain-their-rules-the-growing-patchwork-of-neural-data-regulation) · [UNESCO adopts first global framework on neurotechnology ethics — UNESCO UK](https://unesco.org.uk/news/unesco-adopts-first-global-framework-on-neurotechnology-ethics)

# neuralnanorobotics

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


also “brain/cloud interface (B/CI)”

A proposal, published in Frontiers in Neuroscience in 2019, for future nanoscale robots that would travel through the bloodstream, cross the [blood–brain barrier](/glossary/blood-brain-barrier), position themselves among or inside brain cells, and link the brain wirelessly to cloud computing in real time. The authors called the result a brain/cloud interface and suggested it might be possible within 20 to 30 years.

It is a proposal. Nothing of the kind has been built or demonstrated, and the nanorobots it describes do not exist.

The same paper uses the phrase “transparent shadowing” for experiencing parts of another person’s life through such an interface. It is unrelated to [transparent brain-computer interfaces](/glossary/transparent-brain-computer-interfaces-bcis), which are named for a see-through material.

Related terms: [blood–brain barrier](/glossary/blood-brain-barrier), [brain-data-as-a-service](/glossary/brain-data-as-a-service-brain-spacehttp-brain-space), [telepathy](/glossary/telepathy), [cognitive liberty](/glossary/cognitive-liberty)

Sources: [Human Brain/Cloud Interface — Martins et al., Frontiers in Neuroscience 13:112 (2019)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6450227/)

# Neuro-engagement

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


NOOR·oh·en·GAYJ·muhnt

A term used in this work for a process in which an external party establishes a brain-to-brain connection with a person — through Diving — and the target participant comes to experience the physical sensations of the orchestrator who initiated the connection. It is the channel through which such sensations, and other signals, are sent to or read from the participant's nervous system.

No capability of this kind appears in the published scientific record. Every documented system for reading or writing neural signals requires contact with the head, equipment the person is wearing, or hours of training with a cooperative subject. The term is retained because the work uses it; it is a term of art within this archive, not an established mechanism. See [What would it actually take to do this without consent?](/concepts#what-it-would-take).

Related terms: [Diving](/glossary/diving), [Breaching](/glossary/breaching), [Phantom sensations](/glossary/phantom-sensations), [No-touch torture](/glossary/no-touch-torture)

# neuro-forensics

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


NOOR·oh·fuh·REN·ziks

The use of neuroscience methods, such as EEG-based “brain fingerprinting”, fMRI lie detection and brain imaging, to answer legal questions about memory, deception or responsibility.

Courts have not accepted these tests as reliable proof. In United States v. Semrau (2012), a federal appeals court upheld the exclusion of fMRI lie-detection evidence, finding no known error rate outside the laboratory. In 2010 India's Supreme Court held that forcing a person to undergo such brain tests violates the right against self-incrimination.

Some reference material in this record describes “Neuro-Forensic Units” at the Colorado Attorney General's office and the King County Prosecutor. No public evidence of units by that name was found (checked 1 October 2026).

The author’s [Personal Protection Plan](/concepts/personal-protection-plan) uses the phrase “neural audit” for a set of steps: a lawyer, an independent technical check, a complaint and health care. No single organisation offers a neural audit as a service today.

In June 2025 the American Medical Association adopted a policy on safeguarding neural data collected by neurotechnologies.

Related terms: [electroencephalogram](/glossary/electroencephalogram), [brain–computer interface](/glossary/braincomputer-interface-bci), [cognitive liberty](/glossary/cognitive-liberty), [Colorado Privacy Act](/glossary/colorado-privacy-act)

Sources: [United States v. Semrau, 6th Cir. 2012 — Justia](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/ca6/11-5396/11-5396-2012-09-07.html) · [Selvi v. State of Karnataka, Supreme Court of India, 2010](https://www.manupatracademy.com/LegalPost/MANU_SC_0325_2010) · [Using Imaging to Identify Deceit — American Academy of Arts & Sciences](https://www.amacad.org/publication/using-imaging-identify-deceit-scientific-and-ethical-questions/section/8) · [American Medical Association adopts landmark policy on neural data privacy — Cooley](https://www.cooley.com/news/insight/2025/2025-07-01-american-medical-association-adopts-landmark-policy-on-neural-data-privacy)

# neuroethics

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


NOOR·oh·eth·iks

The study of the ethical, legal and social questions raised by brain science, and by technology that can read from or act on the brain and nervous system. The field took its name in 2002, at a conference in San Francisco called "Neuroethics: Mapping the Field".

Its central concerns are consent, mental privacy and freedom of thought: whether anyone may record, decode or influence what happens in a person's mind without their knowing, voluntary agreement. Those principles are now reaching law and policy. Colorado added neural data to its privacy law in 2024, and in November 2025 UNESCO's member states adopted the first global standard on the ethics of neurotechnology.

Related terms: [cognitive liberty](/glossary/cognitive-liberty), [neurotechnology](/glossary/neurotechnology), [neural privacy](/glossary/neural-privacy), [Colorado Privacy Act](/glossary/colorado-privacy-act), [Nuremberg Code](/glossary/nuremberg-code)

Sources: [UNESCO adopts first global framework on neurotechnology ethics — UNESCO UK](https://unesco.org.uk/news/unesco-adopts-first-global-framework-on-neurotechnology-ethics)

# neurotechnology

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


NOOR·oh·tek·NOL·uh·jee

Any technology that records, reads or changes activity in the brain or nervous system. It ranges from medical devices such as cochlear implants, deep brain stimulators and EEG, to consumer headbands that measure brain waves, to brain–computer interfaces that let a person control a computer with brain signals alone.

Because brain activity can reveal health, mood and intention, international bodies have set standards for its use: the OECD in 2019, and UNESCO, whose member states adopted the first global standard on the ethics of neurotechnology in November 2025. Several US states now protect neural data in privacy law.

Related terms: [brain–computer interface](/glossary/braincomputer-interface-bci), [neural interface](/glossary/neural-interface), [neural decoding](/glossary/neural-decoding), [neuroethics](/glossary/neuroethics), [neural privacy](/glossary/neural-privacy)

Sources: [UNESCO adopts first global framework on neurotechnology ethics — UNESCO UK](https://unesco.org.uk/news/unesco-adopts-first-global-framework-on-neurotechnology-ethics)

# No-touch torture

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


noh·tuhch TOR·cher

Coercion or harm inflicted without physical contact — such as sensory deprivation or overload, sleep deprivation, isolation, and psychological manipulation. A recognized concept in interrogation and human-rights literature. In this work, it is understood to be delivered through Neuro-engagement — the brain-to-brain channel by which sensations and signals can be imposed on a person's nervous system with no physical contact at all.

Related terms: [Neuro-engagement](/glossary/neuro-engagement), [Directed-energy](/glossary/directed-energy), [torment](/glossary/torment), [Zersetzung tactics](/glossary/zersetzung-tactics)

# non-ionizing radiation

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


Electromagnetic radiation with enough energy to move or heat atoms but not enough to strip their electrons (to “ionize”). It includes radio waves, microwaves, infrared, visible light, and lower-frequency ultraviolet — and is the basis of all wireless communication. Radio-frequency signals can also be used for sensing: research systems use ordinary Wi-Fi and radar (both non-ionizing) to detect motion and even coarse human figures through walls. Contrast with ionizing radiation (X-rays, gamma rays), which carries enough energy to damage DNA.

Related terms: [electromagnetic field](/glossary/electromagnetic-field), [Directed-energy](/glossary/directed-energy), [microwave auditory effect](/glossary/microwave-auditory-effect), [hardware](/glossary/hardware-computer), [optogenetics](/glossary/optogenetics)

Sources: [Wi-Vi: seeing through walls with Wi-Fi — MIT](https://people.csail.mit.edu/fadel/wivi/); [Household radar sees through walls — IEEE Spectrum](https://spectrum.ieee.org/household-radar-can-see-through-walls-and-knows-how-youre-feeling)

# Nuremberg Code

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


NOOR·uhm·burg kohd

A ten-point statement of medical research ethics, issued on 19 August 1947 by an American military [tribunal](/glossary/tribunal) at the close of the Doctors' Trial. In that trial, German physicians and medical administrators were tried for war crimes and crimes against humanity committed during the Holocaust.

Its first point is the one most often cited: the voluntary consent of the human subject is absolutely essential. A participant must have the legal capacity to consent, be free of coercion or deception, and be told the nature, duration, purpose, methods, risks and likely effects on their health before agreeing.

Related terms: [cognitive liberty](/glossary/cognitive-liberty), [obedience experiment](/glossary/obedience-experiment), [no-touch torture](/glossary/no-touch-torture), [International Criminal Court](/glossary/international-criminal-court)

Sources: [The Nuremberg Code — Holocaust Encyclopedia, United States Holocaust Memorial Museum](https://encyclopedia.ushmm.org/content/en/article/the-nuremberg-code)

# obedience experiment

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


the Milgram experiment

A landmark series of social-psychology studies conducted by Stanley Milgram at Yale University beginning in 1961, testing how far ordinary people would obey an authority figure instructing them to harm another person. Participants were directed to administer what they believed were increasingly severe electric shocks to a “learner” for wrong answers, while an experimenter insisted they continue. In reality the “learner” was a confederate (an actor), and no real shocks were ever delivered — but the participants believed the shocks were genuine, which is what the study measured.

Core finding: roughly two-thirds of participants (about 65%) continued to the highest, apparently lethal 450-volt level simply because an authority figure told them to — even while showing visible distress. Beforehand, psychiatrists had predicted only about 0.1% would go that far. The result overturned the assumption that only unusual or “evil” people inflict serious harm, showing instead that the situation — pressure from a legitimate-seeming authority — can override individual character. Milgram explained this through the “agentic state”: under authority, people shift moral responsibility upward and stop seeing themselves as the author of their own actions. The effect has proven robust across cultures and decades, with modern replications still finding 70–90% compliance.

Why it matters: the studies offer a framework for how ordinary people come to take part in institutional harm — from the conduct of Nazi units (Milgram framed the work partly in response to the Holocaust and the Eichmann trial) to everyday cases of people obeying an illegitimate “authority.” The controversy over the distress it caused participants also helped create the modern human-subjects protection system (informed consent, review boards). Its reach is broad: it is cited across law and the social sciences, and — per the Association for Psychological Science — the U.S. Army has required cadets to study it and its implications.

Limitations: the studies are also heavily debated — later archival work (Perry, 2013) found the experimenters sometimes improvised pressure beyond the written script, many participants may have doubted the shocks were real, and the sample was all-male and self-selected.

Related terms: [cognitive liberty](/glossary/cognitive-liberty), [Gang stalking](/glossary/gang-stalking), [Zersetzung tactics](/glossary/zersetzung-tactics)

Sources: [The Obedience Experiments at 50 — Association for Psychological Science](https://www.psychologicalscience.org/observer/online-exclusive-the-obedience-experiments-at-50); [Milgram experiment — Britannica](https://www.britannica.com/science/Milgram-experiment); [Milgram experiment: summary & legacy — SimplyPsychology](https://www.simplypsychology.org/milgram.html)

Further viewing: [Experimenter (2015 film on Stanley Milgram)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=o8XriePFEug)

# optogenetics

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


A research technique that genetically modifies specific neurons so that they switch on or off in response to light. It lets scientists control chosen brain cells precisely, and is used widely in animal research.

Ordinarily it needs an optical fibre implanted in the brain, because visible light does not pass through the skull.

Documented real-world example: in 2018, a team led by RIKEN in Japan used upconversion [nanoparticles](/glossary/nanoparticle), which turn near-infrared light that can pass through tissue into visible light, to trigger neurons deep in the brains of mice from outside the head, without an implanted fibre. The mice were genetically modified and the nanoparticles were injected into the brain.

Related terms: [nanoparticle](/glossary/nanoparticle), [neural interface](/glossary/neural-interface), [non-ionizing radiation](/glossary/non-ionizing-radiation)

Sources: [Near-infrared deep brain stimulation via upconversion nanoparticle–mediated optogenetics — RIKEN press release (2018)](https://www.riken.jp/en/news_pubs/research_news/pr/2018/20180209_1/)

# parametric array

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


also “audio spotlight”, “directional sound beam”

A loudspeaker that projects a narrow beam of sound rather than filling a room. It emits an ultrasonic carrier — typically around 40 kHz, well above hearing — modulated with the audio signal. Air is slightly non-linear, so as the beam travels the ultrasound self-demodulates and audible sound appears along its path.

Its behaviour is the closest documented match to descriptions of a voice only one person can hear. MIT’s Media Lab, where the commercial version was invented, describes it plainly: aimed at a listener it “allows only that person to hear it”, and aimed at a surface it “causes the sound to appear to originate from there, creating something of a ‘virtual loudspeaker’”.

Documented real-world example: Holosonics has sold the Audio Spotlight commercially since 2000. The AS-24iX is rated at 95 dB SPL at 1 kHz and listed by resellers at about $2,299. Off the beam axis, level falls below a tenth of the on-axis amplitude.

Its limits matter as much as its capabilities. Demodulation loss is around 60 dB, air absorbs roughly 1 dB per metre at these frequencies, bass is poorly directional, and anything blocking the beam blocks the sound. The audible result is real sound in air: a second person standing inside the beam hears it, and a microphone inside the beam records it.

Related terms: [structure-borne audio](/glossary/structure-borne-audio), [the Mosquito](/glossary/mosquito-device), [microwave auditory effect](/glossary/microwave-auditory-effect), [Voice-to-skull](/glossary/voice-to-skull)

Sources: [Audio Spotlight — MIT Media Lab](https://www.media.mit.edu/projects/audio-spotlight/overview/); [Products — Holosonics](https://www.holosonics.com/products-x); [Sound from ultrasound — Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sound_from_ultrasound)

# Phantom sensations

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


FAN·tuhm sen·SAY·shuhns

Sensations perceived without a corresponding external physical stimulus. Clinically associated with phantom-limb phenomena — where an amputee still feels a missing limb — and in this work extended to sensations experienced without any physical source, including those induced externally.

Phantom sensations can arise across all five senses: sight (light, images, or movement that isn't there), hearing (sounds, tones, or voices with no acoustic source — see Voice-to-skull), smell (phantom odors), taste (phantom flavors), and touch (pressure, heat, pain, vibration, or movement felt without contact). In the framework of this archive, such experiences are described as being prompted through Neuro-engagement — the brain-to-brain channel by which an external orchestrator can transmit a sensation directly to a participant's nervous system, so that the participant perceives it as their own.

Related terms: [Neuro-engagement](/glossary/neuro-engagement), [Voice-to-skull](/glossary/voice-to-skull), [Directed-energy](/glossary/directed-energy)

# piezoelectricity

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


pie-EE-zo-ee-lek-TRISS-ih-tee · from the Greek piezein, to press

The property of certain crystals and ceramics — quartz, and engineered materials such as PZT — of producing an electrical charge when squeezed, and of changing shape when a voltage is applied across them. The two directions have names. The direct effect, pressure to charge, is how a piezoelectric sensor works. The converse effect, voltage to movement, is how a piezoelectric speaker or actuator works.

Bidirectional does not mean interchangeable, and this is the point most often missed. A sensing or energy-harvesting element is wired into a rectifier and a storage circuit. A sound-producing element needs an audio-band signal source and a high-voltage drive amplifier. Those two circuits are opposites. An element built for one cannot perform the other without being rebuilt around it.

Documented real-world example: piezoelectric strips are a standard roadway sensor class listed by the US Federal Highway Administration for vehicle counting and weigh-in-motion, sold commercially by International Road Dynamics and TE Connectivity. The Colorado Department of Transportation operates weigh-in-motion at highway stations including Limon, Trinidad, Monument and Lamar, upgrading through autumn 2026. Where piezoelectric elements are in roads, they are there to read traffic — not to generate power, and not to produce sound.

Related terms: [energy harvesting](/glossary/energy-harvesting), [structure-borne audio](/glossary/structure-borne-audio), [microwave auditory effect](/glossary/microwave-auditory-effect), [neural dust](/glossary/neural-dust)

Sources: [Traffic Monitoring Guidebook, chapter 4 — FHWA](https://www.fhwa.dot.gov/clas/ctip/traffic_monitoring_guidebook/ch_4.aspx); [Piezoelectric sensors — International Road Dynamics](https://irdinc.com/products/wim-systems/piezoelectric-sensors/); [Weigh-in-motion project underway in Limon — CDOT](https://www.codot.gov/news/2026/july2026news/weigh-in-motion-project-underway-in-limon)

# presbycusis

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


prez-bih-KYOO-sis · age-related hearing loss

The gradual loss of hearing that comes with age, affecting the highest frequencies first and most severely. It is the reason a tone can be plainly audible to one person and completely inaudible to another standing beside them, with no device and no deception involved.

The slope is continuous rather than a cliff, and the popular claim that nobody over 25 can hear above 16 kHz is not supported by audiometry. In a 2021 study of 162 adults aged 21 to 70, thresholds at 16 kHz averaged about 25 dB HL in the 21–30 group and 50 to 55 dB HL between 31 and 60. In the 61–70 group fewer than one in five gave any response at 16 kHz, and none had a measurable response at 18 kHz. Just over half of those aged 21 to 30 still responded at 20 kHz.

Documented real-world example: this ordinary biology is the entire operating principle of [the Mosquito](/glossary/mosquito-device), an anti-loitering device sold since 2005 that emits roughly 17.4 kHz so that younger people are driven away while older passers-by mostly notice nothing.

Related terms: [the Mosquito](/glossary/mosquito-device), [microwave auditory effect](/glossary/microwave-auditory-effect), [phantom sensations](/glossary/phantom-sensations)

Sources: [Extended high-frequency hearing thresholds by age — Journal of Otolaryngology, Head & Neck Surgery (2021)](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1186/s40463-021-00534-w)

A term used in this work for the cumulative, suffocating pressure created by relentless, inescapable harassment — the sense of being psychologically enveloped or crowded until there is no room to think, rest, or recover. It describes the felt effect of sustained torment, Zersetzung tactics, and No-touch torture, rather than any single act.

# sampling limit

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


also “Nyquist limit”, “codec low-pass”

The ceiling on what a recording can contain, and the reason a recording can be silent about the very thing it was made to capture.

Two separate ceilings apply. The first is arithmetic: a recorder can only represent frequencies below half its sampling rate, so the 44.1 kHz that Android guarantees on every device cannot hold anything above 22.05 kHz, whatever microphone is attached. The second is the compression: lossy formats discard high frequencies deliberately, because most listeners cannot hear them and the bits are better spent elsewhere.

Documented real-world example: Fraunhofer’s AAC encoder — the format behind most phone voice recordings — low-passes at 17,000 Hz at 96 kbps mono, and as low as 13,050 Hz at its lower quality settings. [The Mosquito](/glossary/mosquito-device) emits at roughly 17.4 kHz. A phone recording of a Mosquito can therefore contain nothing at all, with no error, no warning, and a file that plays back as silence. LAME’s MP3 encoder cuts between 16,538 and 19,205 Hz depending on quality; Opus never codes above 20 kHz. Knowles characterises the MEMS microphones used in phones only to 20 kHz, with an uneven response approaching it.

What this means in practice: an uncompressed WAV or PCM recording preserves what the microphone captured; a compressed one may not. And an ultrasonic carrier such as the roughly 40 kHz used by a [parametric array](/glossary/parametric-array) is beyond every ordinary phone, requiring sampling above 120 kHz — dedicated 192 kHz or 250 kHz ultrasonic microphones sell for about £140 to £250.

A failed recording is not evidence that nothing happened. It may only be evidence that the equipment was not built to hear it.

Related terms: [the Mosquito](/glossary/mosquito-device), [parametric array](/glossary/parametric-array), [presbycusis](/glossary/presbycusis), [contact microphone](/glossary/contact-microphone)

Sources: [LAME — HydrogenAudio](https://wiki.hydrogenaudio.org/index.php/LAME); [Fraunhofer FDK AAC — HydrogenAudio](https://wiki.hydrogenaudio.org/index.php?title=Fraunhofer_FDK_AAC); [RFC 6716: Definition of the Opus Audio Codec](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6716.html); [Frequency response and latency of MEMS microphones — Knowles](https://www.knowles.com/docs/default-source/default-document-library/frequency-response-and-latency-of-mems-microphones---theory-and-practice.pdf?sfvrsn=4)

# schizoaffective disorder

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


SKIT·soh·uh·FEK·tiv dis·OR·der

A mental condition that combines a loss of contact with reality, as in schizophrenia, with a mood disorder such as depression or mania. Its exact cause is unknown; genes and brain chemistry may play a role.

There are no medical tests for it. A health care provider assesses the person's mental health, and a psychiatrist may confirm the diagnosis. Invisible Ships never diagnoses anyone and asks people to speak only for themselves.

Related terms: [schizophrenia](/glossary/schizophrenia)

Sources: [Schizoaffective disorder — MedlinePlus (NIH)](https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/000930.htm)

# schizophrenia

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


SKIT·suh·FREE·nee·uh

A serious mental illness that affects how a person thinks, feels and behaves, and can make it hard to tell what is real from what is not. It is usually first diagnosed between the ages of 16 and 30, after a first episode of psychosis.

No medical test confirms it. A psychiatrist makes the diagnosis by examining the person and talking with them, often with their family as well; brain scans and blood tests may be used to rule out other conditions that look similar. Because the diagnosis rests on what a person reports and on how others describe them, Invisible Ships never diagnoses anyone and asks people to speak only for themselves.

Related terms: [schizoaffective disorder](/glossary/schizoaffective-disorder)

Sources: [Schizophrenia — National Institute of Mental Health (NIH)](https://www.nimh.nih.gov/health/topics/schizophrenia); [Schizophrenia — MedlinePlus (NIH)](https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/000928.htm)

# self-elected suicide

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


A phrase used in this record, in a statement recorded on 17 May 2025, for ending one's own life by one's own choice. The record sets it against repeated suggestions that people be pushed toward death.

Colorado: medical aid in dying under the End-of-Life Options Act (2016), for adults with a terminal illness and six months or less to live, who take the medication themselves. No US state lets a clinician administer it. Outside the Act, causing or aiding a suicide is manslaughter in Colorado.

Canada: medical assistance in dying (MAID, since 2016) requires a serious and incurable illness, disease or disability, irreversible decline, and suffering that cannot be relieved; since 2021 death need not be foreseeable, and a clinician may administer it. Financial or social hardship is not a criterion. Mental illness as the only condition is excluded until 17 March 2027: Parliament delayed it twice, in 2023 and 2024, after a joint committee found practitioners concerned about judging whether a mental illness is incurable and telling a request apart from suicidal intent. In 2022 a Veterans Affairs Canada employee raised it with four veterans unprompted; the case was referred to the RCMP.

Elsewhere: some form of assisted dying is legal in fewer than 20 countries, plus most Australian states and 13 US states, covering about 300 million people. In most of the world it is illegal.

If you or someone you know needs support: in the US, call or text 988; elsewhere, findahelpline.com.

Related terms: [euthanasia](/glossary/euthanisia), [Nuremberg Code](/glossary/nuremberg-code), [crimes against humanity](/glossary/crimes-against-humanity)

Sources: [Medical aid in dying — Colorado Department of Public Health and Environment](https://cdphe.colorado.gov/center-for-health-and-environmental-data/registries-and-vital-statistics/medical-aid-in-dying) · [C.R.S. 18-3-104, manslaughter](https://colorado.public.law/statutes/crs_18-3-104) · [Medical assistance in dying — Government of Canada](https://www.canada.ca/en/health-canada/services/health-services-benefits/medical-assistance-dying.html) · [Legislative Summary of Bill C-62 — Library of Parliament](https://lop.parl.ca/sites/PublicWebsite/default/en_CA/ResearchPublications/LegislativeSummaries/441C62E) · [Veterans minister on assisted-dying discussions — Global News](https://globalnews.ca/news/9352616/veterans-affairs-minister-year-end-maid/) · [Where assisted dying is legal — TIME, July 2026](https://time.com/article/2026/07/16/france-assisted-dying-bill-where-legal/)

# structure-borne audio

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


also “surface exciter”, “distributed-mode loudspeaker”

Turning an ordinary surface into a loudspeaker. A small transducer is bonded to a rigid panel — a window, a wall, a table top, a car windscreen — and vibrates it so that the panel itself radiates sound. There is no cone and no visible speaker. The sound appears to come from the surface, or from the room itself.

This is a mature commercial technology rather than a hypothesis, and it is inexpensive.

Documented real-world example: Feonic’s “Whispering Window” mounts to glass with a peel-and-stick adhesive pad, installs in seconds, works on any pane 6 mm or thicker, and is marketed to retailers precisely so that audio reaches passers-by outside the glass. A Dayton Audio DAEX25 exciter pair retails at $32.99 with adhesive pads included. Continental ships the same principle in vehicles under the name Ac2ated Sound, driving the windscreen and trim panels instead of fitting conventional speakers.

Two properties matter for anyone weighing a claim. Every documented method requires physical contact with the surface; there is no evidenced way to drive a window from a distance. And the sound produced is real sound in air — a second person in the room hears it, and an ordinary microphone records it.

Related terms: [parametric array](/glossary/parametric-array), [piezoelectricity](/glossary/piezoelectricity), [Voice-to-skull](/glossary/voice-to-skull), [microwave auditory effect](/glossary/microwave-auditory-effect)

Sources: [Body sound converter — Monacor](https://www.monacor.com/magazine/body-sound-converter); [Whispering Window — Feonic](https://www.feonic.com/blog/whispering-window/window-speaker-retail-store-sound-system/); [DAEX25 Sound Exciter Pair — Dayton Audio](https://www.daytonaudio.com/product/1087/daex25-sound-exciter-pair); [Ac2ated Sound — Continental](https://www.continental.com/en/press/press-releases/20170628-speakerless-audio/)

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


TAR·gi·tid in·di·VIJ·oo·uhl

A person who reports being subjected to sustained, coordinated surveillance and harassment, often described as involving remote or technological means. Reported methods commonly include gang stalking, directed-energy, and voice-to-skull effects.

# tinnitus

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


TIN·ih·tus or tih·NY·tus · ringing in the ears

The perception of sound that has no external source, so other people cannot hear it. It is usually described as ringing, but also as roaring, buzzing, hissing or clicking, and it can be constant or come and go.

It is common: an estimated 10 to 25% of adults have it. Known causes include noise exposure, hearing loss, some medications, earwax blockage or ear infections, and head or neck injuries. Less often it comes from Ménière's disease, jaw problems, tumours, blood-vessel problems, or conditions such as diabetes and thyroid disorders. Some people develop it for no known reason.

Related terms: [presbycusis](/glossary/presbycusis), [phantom sensations](/glossary/phantom-sensations), [microwave auditory effect](/glossary/microwave-auditory-effect)

Sources: [What Is Tinnitus? — National Institute on Deafness and Other Communication Disorders (NIH)](https://www.nidcd.nih.gov/health/tinnitus)

# transnational repression

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


When a government reaches across its borders to watch, intimidate, threaten or harm people living in other countries, most often dissidents, journalists, activists or members of a diaspora.

Tactics include online harassment, surveillance, threats against family members still in the home country, pressure to return, misuse of international police notices and, in the worst cases, abduction or assassination. The FBI treats it as a crime when it happens in the United States, and Freedom House has documented cases carried out by dozens of governments since 2014.

Related terms: [Zersetzung tactics](/glossary/zersetzung-tactics), [gang stalking](/glossary/gang-stalking), [chilling effect](/glossary/chilling-effect)

Sources: [Transnational repression — FBI](https://www.fbi.gov/investigate/counterintelligence/transnational-repression) · [Transnational repression — Freedom House](https://freedomhouse.org/report/transnational-repression)

# tribunal

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


A court or other body set up to hear a case and decide it. The word is also used more loosely for anything that passes judgment, as in “the tribunal of public opinion.”

The [Nuremberg Code](/glossary/nuremberg-code) came out of one: the American military tribunal that heard the Doctors' Trial and issued the code in its judgment in 1947. A tribunal set up for a single conflict or set of crimes differs from a permanent court such as the [International Criminal Court](/glossary/international-criminal-court).

Related terms: [International Criminal Court](/glossary/international-criminal-court), [Nuremberg Code](/glossary/nuremberg-code)

Sources: [Tribunal — Merriam-Webster](https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/tribunal) · [The Nuremberg Code — Holocaust Encyclopedia, United States Holocaust Memorial Museum](https://encyclopedia.ushmm.org/content/en/article/the-nuremberg-code)

# two-photon microscopy

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


A laser microscope that can image living tissue up to a fraction of a millimetre below the surface with little damage. It works by making fluorescent markers glow only at the exact point where the laser is focused, which keeps the image sharp at depth.

In neuroscience it is used to watch individual neurons at work, almost always in laboratory animals fitted with a small window in the skull. It needs the animal to stay under the microscope, which limits how long and how freely it can be observed.

Documented real-world example: in the UC San Diego study behind [transparent brain-computer interfaces](/glossary/transparent-brain-computer-interfaces-bcis), a two-photon microscope imaged neurons up to 250 micrometres deep, shining its laser through the transparent [graphene](/glossary/graphene) array.

Related terms: [calcium imaging](/glossary/calcium-imaging), [transparent brain-computer interfaces](/glossary/transparent-brain-computer-interfaces-bcis), [graphene](/glossary/graphene)

Sources: [Two-photon excitation microscopy — Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-photon_excitation_microscopy); [Transparent Brain-Computer Interface Uses AI and Nanotech — Psychology Today (Cami Rosso, 24 Jan 2024)](https://www.psychologytoday.com/us/blog/the-future-brain/202401/transparent-brain-computer-interface-uses-ai-and-nanotech)

# Voice-to-skull (V2K)

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


voyss·too·skuhl · synthetic telepathy

A claimed method of transmitting brain–computer interface data — signals sent to the brain — remotely and with no implanted device, such that a person perceives audible speech or sound inside the head with no external acoustic source. It describes the “write” (input) side of a BCI delivered at a distance: where cochlear and auditory brainstem implants create sound by directly stimulating neural tissue through surgically placed electrodes, V2K describes achieving a comparable internal perception wirelessly. Its proposed physical basis is the microwave auditory effect; claims of transmitting intelligible speech this way go beyond what that documented effect has established.

Related terms: [microwave auditory effect](/glossary/microwave-auditory-effect), [telepathy](/glossary/telepathy), [Phantom sensations](/glossary/phantom-sensations), [Targeted individual](/glossary/targeted-individual), [Directed-energy](/glossary/directed-energy), [brain–computer interface](/glossary/braincomputer-interface-bci)

Sources: [Auditory brainstem implant — Cleveland Clinic](https://my.clevelandclinic.org/health/treatments/22723-auditory-brainstem-implant); [Evoking artificial speech perception through invasive brain stimulation — PMC/NIH](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11234843/)

Serious violations of the laws of armed conflict, defined in Article 8 of the Rome Statute, such as wilful killing, torture or deliberately attacking civilians. They can only be committed during an armed conflict, international or internal.

Related terms: [crimes against humanity](/glossary/crimes-against-humanity), [atrocities](/glossary/atrocities), [International Criminal Court](/glossary/international-criminal-court), [tribunal](/glossary/tribunal)

# Zersetzung tactics

*Site-authored. This term was written for invisibleships.com to explain the work; it is not extracted from the source document series, and it carries no source-document id for that reason. Terms drawn from the primary record are in `glossary/` and do carry one.*


tsair·ZET·soong

From the German for “decomposition.” A specific, documented strategy of covert psychological attrition — disruption, gaslighting, isolation, and manufactured setbacks — used to quietly destabilize and discredit a targeted person without overt force; developed and used by the East German Stasi. Where gang stalking is a vernacular term for the general pattern, Zersetzung is a defined, historically documented methodology. In this work, such tactics are applied and amplified through neuro-technology.

Documented real-world example: eBay's 2019 campaign against journalists David and Ina Steiner of Massachusetts — coordinated harassment and surveillance meant to intimidate and silence critics. Seven eBay employees were federally charged with cyberstalking; several were convicted and imprisoned, eBay paid a $3 million criminal penalty under a deferred-prosecution agreement, and the company settled the Steiners' civil suit for $55.7 million (2026).

Related terms: [Gaslighting](/glossary/gaslighting), [torment](/glossary/torment), [psychological smothering](/glossary/psychological-smothering), [No-touch torture](/glossary/no-touch-torture), [Gang stalking](/glossary/gang-stalking), [Targeted individual](/glossary/targeted-individual)

Sources: [Zersetzung — Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zersetzung); [United States v. eBay Inc. — U.S. DOJ](https://www.justice.gov/usao-ma/case/us-v-ebay-inc)

# About the author

*As published on invisibleships.com/author. `IS_META_about-author.md`, also
in this folder, is a verbatim extract of the original document series and is
a different, earlier text.*

Invisible Ships is a firsthand, in-progress record of what its author describes as neuro-tech terrorism — a daily journal of dated entries and verbatim transcripts alongside technical analysis, spanning 2,800+ pages. It documents external communications and events as they were experienced, preserved as evidence, with the author's copyright and Critical Disclaimer carried throughout.

I'm Sean C. Harris, a UX and product designer in Denver with more than 12 years in technology. I'm a father and a martial-arts black belt. Since fall 2024 I have kept a daily record of communications and events I've experienced. Invisible Ships is that record: dated journal entries, transcripts and technical analysis.

The sections below are here so readers can check who I am for themselves. Damaging a person's reputation is a documented part of [Zersetzung tactics](https://www.invisibleships.com/glossary/zersetzung-tactics), and the links are there so anyone can verify these facts directly.

# Category & Tag Taxonomy

This file is the controlled vocabulary used across the corpus. Every chunk's YAML header draws its `categories`, `statement_types`, and (optionally) `themes` from these lists so the website and any AI tool can filter consistently.

## Structural categories (`categories`)

- **journal** — a dated journal day or its recordings.
- **transcript** — a recording chunk containing captured statements.
- **entry** — a day-level index chunk.
- **front-matter** — copyright, disclaimer, about-author, taxonomy.
- **methodology** — how transcripts are captured and processed.
- **findings** — analytical findings / executive summary material.
- **evidence** — references to physical/possible evidence.
- **glossary** — a defined term.
- **reference** — a standalone reference/analysis document section.
- **protection** — personal/household protection guidance.
- **legal** — legal strategy, prosecution, affidavit, justice material.
- **analysis** — AI/LLM analysis of statements and patterns.

## Statement types (`statement_types`)

The author's own annotation convention, preserved verbatim from the transcripts:

- **from-the-distance** — external statements from unknown communicators. Bound by the Critical Disclaimer; these are NOT the author's views.
- **from-the-author** — Sean's own responses, questions, or speculation.
- **paraphrasing** — the author's approximate capture when exact wording could not be recorded.

## Thematic tags (`themes`) — reserved for enrichment

Derived from the *LLM Analysis of External Statements* document. Not auto-applied by the deterministic pipeline; reserved for a later tagging pass and available to the website as a filter axis.

- **euthanization-violence** — statements pressuring self-harm/euthanization or threatening violence.
- **psychological-siege** — gaslighting, behavior control, confusion tactics.
- **incrimination-panic** — reactive/confessional statements implicating groups.
- **suppression-financial** — attempts to block work or financial support.
- **information-control** — attempts to censor searches/reading.
- **technology-context** — references to BCI, AI voice cloning, off-site locations.

## How tags are applied

`categories` and `statement_types` are assigned deterministically by the conversion pipeline (structure + text markers). `themes` and `location` are best-effort and can be refined in the database without regenerating the Markdown.

# Content warning, and a note on safety

*Shown to every reader before entry. It applies to the whole archive and to
every file in this download.*

**Content warning: this archive contains sensitive and potentially disturbing material.**

## A Note on Safety

This archive documents communications received without consent, including material that references coercion, self-harm, and euthanasia. These materials are preserved as documentation of external statements. They do not reflect the author's beliefs, and the author does not endorse, encourage, or condone self-harm or harm to anyone.

If this material feels distressing, please step away and return only when you feel ready. You may also wish to speak with someone you trust.

If you are in the United States and are thinking about harming yourself, call or text 988 to reach the Suicide & Crisis Lifeline. If you are elsewhere, contact local emergency or crisis-support services.

For practical guidance about protecting your personal space and well-being, see the Personal Protection Plan in the Documents section.

# Why “Invisible Ships”

*Shown to every reader on the way into the archive. It is the archive's
account of its own name and its own premise.*

## Perceptual set

Perceptual set is a well-documented principle in cognitive psychology: our expectations, prior experience, context, and mental frameworks shape what we perceive — and can keep us from registering something we have no concept for. We tend to notice what we are prepared to notice.

The work takes its name from an old, often-told story. When European ships first appeared off certain coastlines, it is said the people onshore barely reacted — the vessels lay so far outside anything they had seen that they struggled to register them at all, until someone who studied the strange disturbance on the water learned to see them, and taught others where to look.

**The anecdote is almost certainly apocryphal. The principle it dramatizes is not — perceptual set is real and measurable.**

This archive is an attempt to make something visible: to describe, in plain and dated detail, a phenomenon that is easy to look past precisely because most people have no framework for it yet.

---
